# Security & Audits

## Core Security Mechanisms

## 1. Decentralization

At its core, Analog is secured by an [NPoS-powered Timechain](/documentation/developers/protocol-overview#timechain). The Timechain is designed to have no single point of failure, primarily through decentralization. Time Nodes are run by individuals or entities in a permissionless manner. All the inbound/outbound messages and decisions made through state changes are validated on-chain, and the authored blocks are immutable, verifiable, and always available.

### 2. Threshold Signature Schemes (TSS)

To effect state changes on external chains, Timechain authorizes a single [shard](/documentation/resources/glossary#shard) that utilizes [FROST TSS ](https://eprint.iacr.org/2020/852.pdf)to sign messages, mitigating the risks inherent in a centralized signing key. No single node operator or individual entity has access to the complete private at any point in time. Although certain aspects of the Chronicle Nodes network are not fully decentralized at present, we plan to progressively decentralize this in future iterations.

### 3. Key Rotations

The Timechain employs a key rotation mechanism to enhance security. Periodically, the protocol churns out some Chronicle Nodes from their [shards](/documentation/resources/glossary#shard) and replaces them with standby nodes. The new shard then generates a new TSS key, ensuring only a supermajority of Chronicle Nodes within that shard can participate in message signing.

## Timechain Safe Mode

Analog, like any complex distributed system, is designed with the understanding that not everything will function as intended, even with careful precautions. Timechain Safe Mode—an inbuilt redundancy feature—allows Time Nodes to temporarily suspend most core protocol functions in response to potential security threats or instabilities.

When activated, the safe mode allows validators to continue authoring blocks, processing key extrinsics and events, while significantly halting other protocol tasks like key rotations, staking claims, and cross-chain requests.

## Open Source

In the future, we will make Analog’s core repositories fully open source, allowing the community to freely contribute to the protocol’s development.

## Audits

Analog has thoroughly been audited by [Hacken,](https://hacken.io/) with more audits from third-party companies to come. The auditing report can be found [here](https://audits.hacken.io/analog/l1-analog-node-sdk-chain-dec2023/).


# FAQ

Find answers to frequently asked questions below. If your question isn't listed, don’t hesitate to ask it on our [Discord](https://discord.com/invite/analog)!

## Fundamentals

<details>

<summary><strong>What is Analog?</strong></summary>

Analog is a sovereign, interoperability-native blockchain designed to unify fragmented blockchain ecosystems. It has positioned itself as Web3’s interoperability backbone, combining a sovereign blockchain, AI-driven tools, and a developer-friendly stack to unify fragmented ecosystems. For builders, it offers a unique value proposition: it simplifies cross-chain development, accesses deeper liquidity, and pioneers the tokenization of real-world assets.&#x20;

Analog started as a suite of interoperability protocols built on the Substrate SDK, which underpins the Polkadot blockchain. However, this vision has evolved into a dedicated blockchain focused on interoperability, liquidity, and AI-driven automation. This shift allows Analog to:

* Act as a neutral hub for cross-chain communication and asset mobility.
* Optimize validator management, governance, and transaction efficiency through its sovereign blockchain  (i.e., [Timechain](#what-is-timechain)).&#x20;
* Integrate AI to enhance DeFi, governance, and trading.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What is the significance of the Analog world in relation to the blockchain ecosystem?</strong></summary>

Analog’s suite of interoperability tools addresses critical pain points in Web3, such as:

1. **Fueling DeFi & RWAs:** By enabling cross-chain memecoins, tokenized real estate, and supply chain assets, Analog bridges TradFi and Web3.
2. **Unifying Liquidity:** Analog eliminates fragmented liquidity, letting assets flow freely between chains (e.g., Solana to Ethereum).
3. **Omni-Chain AI:** Integrating AI into Analog’s suite of tools optimizes liquidity pools, governance decisions, and trading strategies in real-time.
4. **Cross-Chain Swaps:** Products like [Zenswap](https://zenswap.io/) use AI-driven pricing to minimize slippage and improve swap efficiency.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What are the essential elements of the Analog tech stack?</strong></summary>

Analog tech stack consists of the following key components:

* **Timechain**: Analog’s core blockchain (built on Substrate) acts as an accountability layer for cross-chain activities. It manages validators, governance, and transaction finality.&#x20;
* **Analog GMP:** A secure protocol for sending data, tokens, and orchestrating smart contract actions across chains. It lets developers build cross-chain dApps (e.g., a DEX that aggregates liquidity from Ethereum and Solana).
* A**nalog Automation (coming soon):** A decentralized platform for triggering cross-chain actions (e.g., auto-rebalancing a portfolio on Astar when Ethereum price drops).
* **Analog Watch:** A data toolkit that comprises the SDK and Portal for querying on-chain data across various blockchains

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What are the key benefits for developers choosing to build on Analog?</strong></summary>

As a developer, you should consider building your product on Analog because of the following reasons:

* **Cross-chain simplicity**: [Analog GMP ](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp#quickstart)allows you to build dApps that communicate arbitrary messages and transfer tokens between chains without needing custom bridges. You can also build dApps that natively interact with Ethereum, Solana, TON, etc.
* **Liquidity access:** Analog’s suite of products enables your dApp to access unified liquidity pools across chains, decreasing reliance on isolated ecosystems.
* **Built-in RWA infrastructure:** You can tokenize real-world assets (RWA) with built-in compliance and cross-chain liquidity solutions.
* **Future-proofing:** You can leverage Analog’s focus on cross-chain interoperability and AI to ensure your dApp remains at the forefront of DeFi and governance trends.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What entities make up the Analog network or ecosystem?</strong></summary>

The Analog ecosystem is a comprehensive network featuring a range of dApps, blockchains, and node operators, all supported by an active open-source developer community. The Analog Network empowers notable DeFi apps like [Zenswap](https://zenswap.io/) and [Pixelport](https://pixelport.xyz/), which are crucial for enabling seamless cross-chain marketplace functionalities.&#x20;

While currently operating under a permissioned model, Analog's long-term vision is to become a permissionless protocol, democratizing access and allowing anyone to contribute to its expanding ecosystem. See the [Collaborators](https://www.analog.one/) page for more details.&#x20;

<br>

</details>

## Products

<details>

<summary><strong>What is Timechain?</strong></summary>

Timechain is a sovereign blockchain developed using the Substrate SDK. It functions as the core component of the Analog Network and serves as an accountability layer designed to oversee and secure cross-chain interactions.&#x20;

In a way, Timechain serves as the central hub in Analog's architecture, linking multiple blockchains (spokes). This streamlines cross-chain communication by directing all messages and data through Timechain, minimizing complexity and boosting security. For further details, refer to the [Protocol Overview.](/documentation/developers/protocol-overview)&#x20;

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What is Timechain EVM, and how it it different from the Timechain?</strong></summary>

Timechain EVM is an embedded Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM) within the Analog Timechain. It lets developers **deploy and run Ethereum-compatible smart contracts** (e.g., Solidity) natively, combining Ethereum’s tooling with the Timechain’s security and performance.&#x20;

**Timechain** is Analog's core decentralized network. It's built on Substrate and handles all cross-chain interactions, asset transfers, and message execution between different blockchains. Think of it as the foundational layer for secure and scalable cross-chain communication.

**Timechain EVM** is a specialized component *within* the Timechain. While the Timechain manages the overall cross-chain logic and network security, the Timechain EVM specifically provides the environment for executing Ethereum smart contracts directly on the Timechain, without needing external or bridged EVMs.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>How does Timechain EVM stack up against competing EVM solutions?</strong></summary>

Timechain EVM provides a powerful and efficient environment for smart contracts, offering several benefits over traditional EVM-compatible chains:

* **Native Integration:** Timechain EVM is built directly into the Timechain, not as a separate component. This means EVM contracts can smoothly interact with Timechain's core features, making operations more efficient and versatile.
* **Superior Performance:** Powered by **PolkaVM**, Timechain EVM executes smart contracts significantly faster. PolkaVM's just-in-time (JIT) compilation and register optimization lead to near-native execution speeds and lower operational costs.
* **Familiar Developer Experience:** You can keep using your favorite Ethereum development tools like **Remix**, **Hardhat**, and **Foundry**. You'll enjoy the same familiar workflow while benefiting from Timechain's enhanced performance and advanced features.
* **Enhanced Interoperability:** Running EVM contracts natively within Timechain simplifies cross-chain operations. This reduces the need for external bridges, resulting in more secure and flexible movement of assets and liquidity across different blockchain networks.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What is Analog GMP?</strong></summary>

[Analog GMP](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp) is a cross-chain communication protocol that enables interoperability between disparate blockchains. It allows developers to build dApps that operate seamlessly across multiple networks. Unlike single-purpose bridges, Analog GMP is protocol-agnostic, supporting any blockchain or use case (e.g., DeFi, NFTs, DAOs, etc.).

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What is Analog Watch?</strong></summary>

[Analog Watch ](/documentation/developers/analog-watch)is the data primitive that transforms raw blockchain data into actionable insights, enabling developers and users to navigate and leverage the interconnected ecosystem that Analog creates. It indexes data across blockchains, allowing developers and users to query transactions, events, and states from any supported chain in a unified format.&#x20;

For example, developers and users can track liquidity pools and asset movements across chains in real-time, providing insights for cross-chain DEXs, arbitrage bots, and automated trading strategies.

</details>

## Token Economics

<details>

<summary><strong>What is the economic model underpinning Analog’s operations?</strong></summary>

The economic model underpinning Analog’s operations is a multi-faceted token-driven ecosystem designed to incentivize participation, secure the network, and facilitate seamless cross-chain interoperability. The model revolves around the ANLOG token, which is the linchpin for staking, transaction fees, governance, and protocol functionality.&#x20;

For more details, refer to the [Tokenomics paper.](https://docs.google.com/document/u/2/d/e/2PACX-1vRCpMd7VmDxqGYXYZfj-1RyHdWRU6DfBf1tTad4vlpmmvp4Coavwh9mmT42Te665A/pub)&#x20;

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>How can I buy $ANLOG token?</strong></summary>

$ANLOG is available on exchanges such as Bitget, MEXC Global, KuCoin, and Gate.io. For details, visit "[Where to Buy ANLOG](https://docs.analog.one/documentation/resources/tokenomics/where-to-buy-anlog)".

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>How do I get testnet tokens (i.e., TANLOG)?</strong></summary>

Testnet tokens (i.e., TANLOG tokens) can be requested through the [Faucet](https://discord.com/invite/analog) channel on our Discord server. Please refer to the [Faucet page](/documentation/analog-network/getting-started/analog-testnet-faucet) for additional information. Keep in mind that TANLOG tokens are designed solely for testing and hold no monetary value.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What utility does $ANLOG serve in the overall Analog ecosystem?</strong></summary>

The ANLOG token is essential to the Analog ecosystem. It serves several key functions, including staking for Time Node validators, earning rewards for block validation, paying transaction fees on the Timechain, acting as collateral for dApps like Analog Watch, and participating in governance decisions.&#x20;

While some Analog products (e.g., [Analog GMP)](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/fee-management) may accept native gas tokens for broader compatibility, ANLOG remains the primary token for the ecosystem's core operations. See [Tokenomics paper ](https://docs.google.com/document/u/2/d/e/2PACX-1vRCpMd7VmDxqGYXYZfj-1RyHdWRU6DfBf1tTad4vlpmmvp4Coavwh9mmT42Te665A/pub)for more details.&#x20;

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What is wrapped ANLOG (wANLOG)?</strong></summary>

wANLOG is an ERC-20 version of the $ANLOG token. It allows users to transfer $ANLOG to the Ethereum blockchain and use it in Ethereum-based DeFi applications despite Analog running its own independent blockchain. For detailed information, see [Tokenomics](https://docs.google.com/document/u/2/d/e/2PACX-1vRCpMd7VmDxqGYXYZfj-1RyHdWRU6DfBf1tTad4vlpmmvp4Coavwh9mmT42Te665A/pub).

</details>

## Analog Testnet & Analog Testnet Points (ATP)

<details>

<summary><strong>What is the Analog Testnet? (And how is it different from the previous "Incentivized Testnet"?)</strong></summary>

The [Analog Testnet](https://testnet.analog.one/) is our live testing environment for the Analog network and its features. Previously, it was referred to as the "**Analog Incentivized Testnet.**" We have updated the name to simply "**Analog Testnet**" to reflect its current primary purpose: a platform for users and developers to beta test new features, identify potential issues, and contribute to the network's stability and performance before mainnet deployment.&#x20;

While past testnet phases involved specific incentives for participation, the focus of the Analog Testnet is now on collaborative testing and development. You can sign up for the Analog Incentivized Testnet [here](https://testnet.analog.one/#/?signup). We've also answered some of the FAQs about the Incentivized Testnet in this link: <https://testnet.analog.one/#/faq>.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What is the current purpose of participating in the Analog Testnet?</strong></summary>

Participation in the [Analog Testnet](https://testnet.analog.one/) is crucial for beta testing new functionalities, helping us gather valuable feedback, and ensuring the robustness of our platform. While it is no longer focused on incentivizing "farmers" through quests for points, active and constructive participation helps shape the future of Analog.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Will there be any more Analog Testnet Points (ATP) airdrops?</strong></summary>

There is one final batch of ATP airdrop scheduled for **Q2 2025**. Specific details and eligibility criteria for this final batch will be announced exclusively on our official [Discord server](https://discord.com/invite/analog).&#x20;

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Does earning or holding Analog Testnet Points (ATP) guarantee me a mainnet $ANLOG token reward?</strong></summary>

No, earning or holding ATP does **not** guarantee a reward, including mainnet $ANLOG tokens or any other form of compensation. ATPs were a measure of participation and contribution during specific past testnet phases. Analog is currently focused on incentivizing mainnet activity and rewarding real users interacting with the live Analog network. Any future reward programs will be communicated clearly through official channels and will likely prioritize mainnet engagement.

</details>

# Analog Watch

**Analog Watch** is a fast, intuitive, and extensible framework for querying on-chain data. It includes two tools—the [**Watch SDK** ](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart)and [**Watch Portal**](https://watch.testnet.analog.one/)—to deploy and retrieve data from supported blockchains. Get started with either tool today via these [environments](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/deployment-environments).

***

## Core Features

* **No-Code Interface:** Publish smart contracts and views effortlessly using Watch Portal’s intuitive UI—no coding required. [Learn more…](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/overview#why-analog-watch)
* **Unified Data with GraphQL:** Query precise data across multiple blockchains and smart contracts using GraphQL’s efficient, flexible API.  [Learn more...](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/overview#why-analog-watch)
* **Multi-Chain Indexing:** Access aggregated data from multiple blockchains and contracts through a single, unified API. [Learn more...](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/deploying-views#problem-overview)
* **Custom Event Data:** Generate tailored datasets from smart contract events. Define contracts and events to index, and the Watch SDK automatically produces structured data. [Learn more…](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/event-data-support)
* **Integrated Authentication & Permissions:** Benefit from built-in Web3 authentication and role-based access control (RBAC) for secure access. [Learn more…](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/overview#why-analog-watch)
* **Smart Contract Automation (Coming Soon):** Automate cross-chain smart contract executions using Watch’s upcoming flexible automation framework.&#x20;

***

# Quickstart

The Watch SDK allows you to interact with Analog Watch in standard Node.js or Web environments.

## Prerequisites

Before using the Watch SDK, ensure the following are set up:

* **Node.js**: [Install](https://nodejs.org/en/download/current) the latest version.
* **Package Manager**: Use **npm** or **yarn**.
* **Substrate-based Wallet**: Required for Analog Watch compatibility. For supported options, see [Supported Wallets](/documentation/analog-network/getting-started/supported-wallets).
* [Node.js](https://nodejs.org/en/download/current) installed (use the latest version).
* **Smart Contract Address**: Browse available contracts on the Analog Watch Testnet or refer to [Listing Smart Contracts](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts) for setup details.

## Installation

Depending on your choice of package manager, you can install the SDK in two ways:

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="npm" %}
{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```bash
npm i @analog-labs/timegraph-js
```

{% endcode %}
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="yarn" %}
{% code lineNumbers="true" %}

```bash
yarn add @analog-labs/timegraph-js
```

{% endcode %}
{% endtab %}
{% endtabs %}

## Authentication

The Watch SDK supports both web and Node.js environments.

* **Web:** Requires the key generation tool (provided by **`timegraph.js`**) and the **`@polkadot/extension-dapp`** library.
* **Node:** Requires the key generation tool and the [`@polkadot/keyring`](https://polkadot.js.org/docs/api/start/keyring/) package.

Authentication follows a three-step process:

### 1. Install extensions and initialize them

Depending on your preferred environment, proceed to generate the SSK as follows:

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="Web" %}
Install [@polkadot/extension-dapp](https://polkadot.js.org/docs/extension/usage) as follows:

{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```typescript
npm i @polkadot/extension-dapp //npm installation for @polkadot/extension-dapp
yarn add @polkadot/extension-dapp //yarn installation for @polkadot/extension-dapp
```

{% endcode %}

Import the `keygen` and `web3FromAdress` and proceed to generate the session keys.&#x20;

{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```typescript
import { keygen } from '@analog-labs/timegraph-js';
import { web3FromAddress } from '@polkadot/extension-dapp';
// init signer based on polkadot/extension-dapp
const { signer } = await web3FromAddress( '<Wallet Address>' );
// pass signer and address to get keygen instance
const _keygen = new keygen({ signer: signer.signRaw,address: '<Wallet Address>'});
// generate api key i.e, role is optional default will be developer                             
const apikey = await _keygen.createApiKey();
//Generate the session key
const sessionKey = await _keygen.createSessionkey();
```

{% endcode %}
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="Node" %}
Install the [@polkadot/Keyring](https://polkadot.js.org/docs/api/start/keyring/) package as follows:

{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```typescript
npm i @polkadot/keyring //npm installation for @polkadot/keyring
yarn add @polkadot/keyring //yarn installation for @polkadot/keyring
```

{% endcode %}

Next, import Keyring and keygen tools and proceed to generate the session keys as follows:

{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import Keyring from "@polkadot/keyring";
import { keygen } from "@analog-labs/timegraph-js";
// init polkadot keypair for node env
const keyring = new Keyring({ type: "sr25519" });
const keypair =  keyring.add...( ... );
//Pass signer and address to get keygen instance
const _keygen = new keygen({ signer: keypair.sign, address: keypair.address });
// generate API key, i.e. role is optional default will be developer
const apiKey = await _keygen.createApiKey( <"Role"> );
// generate session key
const sessionKey = await _keygen.createSessionkey( <"Expiration in ms ");
```

{% endcode %}
{% endtab %}
{% endtabs %}

<details>

<summary><strong>Response</strong></summary>

```json
"apiKey":{
   "key":"HtACKxQe9dx4Xmg8d1sSd7PQnzvNKSS9ViBb2Aq8Fdo",
   "secret":"9yPmWeRLcpGn9rAeqg8npRyBmgA9D8D94KPTpfkD8Kgd",
   "cert":"111CfKkxwN6abhKrnZ4wKEWdpagvfNqe6iUR5Gg....."
},
{
   "ssk":"0;EFVS6JNJ7ah615F69cTHsDrUP2vziy1943hyW6LUbkfr;0;*;1;26528g8cdQfzBw2ue85Hbkcw3cpRmrfrhjhpEScxeKhQuVMYCKzoucNcXUmXEiypg9G7188n9h2pzLCRwD1BFFk3"
}
```

</details>

{% hint style="info" %}
Instead of using the `keygen` and `@polkadot/extension-dapp`/ `@polkadot/Keyring` packages, you can generate the session keys via an inbuilt [Session Key Generator (SKG)](/documentation/resources/tooling/utilities/session-key-generator) toolkit.&#x20;
{% endhint %}

### 2. Create an account

With the session keys generated, you can now create an account by providing the wallet address and SSK to the SDK:

{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from '@analog-labs/timegraph-js';
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: '<URL>', // url to Watch GraphQL instance  
sessionKey: '<SSK>', // session key created by user wallet using SDK keygen
});
const response = timeGraphClient.user.create({ address: '<Wallet Address>' }); 
```

{% endcode %}

<details>

<summary><strong>Response</strong></summary>

```json
"response":{
   {
      "userId":111,
      "wallet":"5GWqFcdT8ugZW8fZhZHQzcx96M2jQYLV7M9KMozYPzLfEdR9",
      "userRole":"developer",
      "subgraph":111
   }
}
```

</details>

### 3. Validate the certificate data

To validate the certificate data for a new API key and save it in storage, you must pass a valid SSK for the signing account as follows:

{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from '@analog-labs/timegraph-js';
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: '<URL>', // url to Watch GraphQL instance 
sessionKey: '<SSK>', // session key created by user wallet using SDK Keygen
});
const response = timeGraphClient.apiKey.certify({ cert: '<certificate>' }); 
```

{% endcode %}

<details>

<summary><strong>Response</strong></summary>

```json
"response":{
   "certifyKey":{
      "userId":100"cert":"111CfKkxwN6abhKrnZ4wKEWdpagvfNqe6iUR5Gg.....",
      "key":"HtACKxQe9dx4Xmg8d1sSd7PQnzvNKSS9ViBb2Aq8Fdo",
      "role":"developer",
      "status":"active"
   }
}
```

</details>

{% hint style="info" %}
**Note:**

You can also enable the previously revoked API key by passing a valid session key for the key's owner like this:

{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from '@analog-labs/timegraph-js';
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: '<URL>', // url to Watch GraphQL instance 
sessionKey: '<SSK>', // session key created by user wallet using SDK keygen
});
const response = timeGraphClient.apiKey.enable({ key: '<Api key>' }); 
```

{% endcode %}

Likewise, you can also revoke an API key by passing a valid session key for the key's owner in the Authorization header:

{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from '@analog-labs/timegraph-js';
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: '<URL>', // url to Watch GraphQL instance 
sessionKey: '<SSK>', // session key created by user wallet using SDK Keygen
});
const response = timeGraphClient.apiKey.revoke({ key: '<Api key>' }); 
```

{% endcode %}
{% endhint %}

```typescript
import { TimegraphClient } from '@analog-labs/timegraph-js';
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: '<https://timegraph.testnet.analog.one/graphql>', // url to Watch GraphQL instance  
sessionKey: '<SSK>', // session key created by user wallet using WASM SDK
});
```

# Overview

Analog Watch is a full-stack indexing and query protocol. Analog Watch makes Web3 data instantly and securely accessible over a GraphQL endpoint, allowing you to build and ship DApps faster. It includes two toolkits:  [Watch SDK](#watch-sdk) and a hosted service [Watch Portal](#watch-portal). &#x20;

{% hint style="warning" %}
Analog Watch will initially operate as a centralized service. However, our long-term goal is to decentralize Web3 data, ensuring it is accessible and trustworthy for all. We plan to achieve this by leveraging the Timechain to create cryptographic proofs (Watch Proofs) that independently verify the authenticity of retrieved data.
{% endhint %}

## Why Analog Watch?

Blockchain data has notable vital features. First, it is ever-growing, and no record can be erased. Second, even though most blockchain data is immutable, the latest blocks can change due to conflicts arising from block finality, chain reorganizations, and uncle'd blocks that need to be resolved.

Long story short, think of blockchain data as consisting of an immutable cold tail (finalized blocks) and a rarely changing head state. The problem that the Analog Watch is solving is that while blockchain networks are great for creating Byzantine Fault Tolerant (BFT) data, they are less efficient when it comes to slicing and dicing it over many axes.

Typically, most smart contracts would only allow you to retrieve data using a related unique identifier. You must know the unique identifier beforehand to query specific details about the smart contract (e.g., a list of operations that have affected an account). Alternatively, you could perform sequential access, traversing every block until you find the specific data element.

To build increasingly complex DApps, you need a reliable Web3 data infrastructure and tooling to process and query blockchain data faster. For example, with the Analog Watch, you can now abstract most of the Web3 data accessibility challenges via a few lines of code and achieve high-level functionalities like [deploying Views ](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/deploying-views)and [querying Views](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/querying-data).&#x20;

Discover the benefits of Analog Watch by exploring [Core Features](/documentation/developers/analog-watch#core-features).

{% hint style="success" %}
To start with Analog Watch, you can utilize the [Watch Portal,](https://watch.testnet.analog.one/) our hosted service, or opt for a comprehensive SDK (i.e., [Watch SDK](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart)). Both solutions ensure the indexed data is easily accessible through a unified GraphQL endpoint, offering the flexibility and power to build DApps.
{% endhint %}

## Watch Portal

The [Watch Portal](https://watch.testnet.analog.one/) is a hosted service that leverages the [Watch SDK ](#watch-sdk)to let you publish and explore smart contracts and View&#x73;**.** It uses pre-built Watch SDK components and drag-and-drop interfaces to allow you to do what you want to do best - build. You can use the Watch Portal to:

1. [Generate API keys to interact with Watch SDK](/documentation/resources/tooling/utilities/session-key-generator).
2. [Deploy and explore the Views](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/deploying-views).
3. [Listing and exploring smart contracts](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts).

## Watch SDK

The [Watch SDK](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart) is a powerful and feature-rich JavaScript toolkit that streamlines the deployment and retrieval of blockchain data. The SDK lets you abstract numerous [challenges](https://www.analog.one/blog/enhancing-data-retrieval-for-dapps) that many developers constantly face when querying Web3 data by simplifying operations, such as:

* [Creating and managing keys.](/documentation/resources/tooling/utilities/session-key-generator)
* [Listing and exploring published smart contracts](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts).
* [Publishing and querying Views](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/querying-data#how-to-query-data-with-the-watch-sdk).


# Architecture

Before we explore the overall architecture of Analog Watch, you might want to understand what a View is.&#x20;

## Core Components

### **1. Immutable Objects**

Data entities like Feeds, Views, Functions, and Automations are stored as content-addressed objects using IPFS hashes. This ensures integrity and versioning:

* **Feeds**: Periodic data collectors (i.e., blockchain data) defined by functions.
* **Views**: SQL-based queries over referenced data sources.
* **Functions**: Self-contained operations that process or transform smart contract data.
* **Automations**: Trigger-action workflows that execute WASM code when feed data changes.

### **2. Decentralized Identity & Access**

* Users/roles (`User`, `Role`) control permissions via cryptographic identities.
* The protocol enforces role-based access (users, developers, admins) for operations.
* The protocol uses **`subgraphs`** to isolate data by user groups for multi-tenancy. You can think of `subgraphs` as organizational units that group related data and functionality.

### **3. Indexing Engine**

* Processes data at fixed intervals (`INDEXING_INTERVAL = 30s`).
* Tracks blockchain data (`BlockIndex`) with clock-based timestamps.
* Charges fees (`INDEXING_FEE`) for data writes to prevent abuse. See [Fees & Rewards ](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/rewards-and-fees)for more details.&#x20;

### **4. Automation System**

* Triggers WebAssembly (WASM) code when feed conditions are met.
* Limits execution depth (`AUTOMATION_DEPTH`) to prevent infinite loops.

## Key Workflows

* **Data Ingestion**:\
  Feeds collect smart contract data (e.g., blockchain events) using user-defined functions. Results are stored in hash-addressed tables.
* **Query Processing**:\
  Views allow SQL queries across multiple immutable datasets. Queries are sanitized to prevent injections and restrict unsafe functions.
* **Balance Tracking**:\
  Users have on-chain balances (`balance()`) and pending adjustments (`unsynced_balance()`), enabling microtransactions for resource usage.
* **Immutable Operations**:\
  All object creations (feeds/views/automations) produce auditable metadata (`ObjectMetadata`) with creator IDs and timestamps.

## How Analog Watch Works

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2F34kswDiYgKNrKZW3vPqL%2FAnalog%20Watch%20Architecture%20(End-to-End).png?alt=media&amp;token=e9ac4564-070a-4669-8706-061032212b30" alt=""><figcaption><p>Analog Watch Architecture: End-to-End Workflow</p></figcaption></figure>

1. Users create [immutable objects](#id-1.-immutable-objects) through an API. Users must be authenticated to deploy these artifacts. Check out [Authentication](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart#authentication) to learn more.&#x20;
2. Objects are assigned unique identifiers and cryptographic hashes.
3. Analog Watch indexes the referenced blockchain data at regular intervals based on the configured clock.
4. Data can be queried through SQL-compatible interfaces via [Watch SDK](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart) or [Watch Portal](https://watch.testnet.analog.one/).
5. Automations can be triggered by data events, creating new data, or executing actions.&#x20;

{% hint style="success" %}
Analog Watch charges fees based on resource usage. You'll pay microtransactions determined by **`INDEXING_FEE`** constants and the resources your operations consume, tracked through your balance. See [Fees & Rewards ](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/rewards-and-fees)for more details.&#x20;

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```rust
pub const INDEXING_FEE: u128 = 50_000; // Per-row fee
pub struct User {
    ...
    pub balance: u128,
    pub unsynced_balance: i128,
}
```

{% endcode %}

All data operations deduct `INDEXING_FEE (50,000 units)` per row processed, with fee validation occurring during query planning. User accounts track two distinct balances:

1. Confirmed Balance (**`balance`**): Settled funds
2. Unsynced Balance (**`unsynced_balance`**): Pending transactions

This dual-balance system enables real-time accounting while supporting eventual consistency across the protocol.
{% endhint %}

### Example Use Case

1. A user defines a Feed to index NFT transfers from Ethereum.
2. The system periodically indexes new transfers into an IPFS-hashed table.
3. A View combines this data with pricing information from another Feed.
4. An Automation triggers a WASM function to send alerts when high-value trades occur.

# Listing Smart Contracts

Learn how to quickly list/deploy new Smart Contracts with the Watch Portal, Watch SDK, and GraphQL IDE.

## Exploring Listed Smart Contracts

The first step of querying or deploying a View on Analog Watch is exploring a list of Smart Contracts that have been published. You can check whether a Smart Contract is listed via these steps:

1. Launch Analog Watch by visiting the [appropriate environment URL ](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/deployment-environments)and connecting your wallet. Once on the page, click the “Library” button. This should display a list of deployed smart contracts as seen in this screen:

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FmQSgN8hrM1sBDcuuuJJK%2FAnalog%20Watch%20Library.png?alt=media&amp;token=1ed7202c-114f-4d02-aa23-c52a05aa8894" alt=""><figcaption><p>Analog Watch Library</p></figcaption></figure>

2. Next, check whether your smart contract(s) and their relevant functions are published by clicking the “Smart Contract” link. This should display a list of all the published smart contracts. To see the details of a particular smart contract, select it by clicking on it. You can also filter a list of smart contracts displayed by specifying relevant tags or searching for a particular contract.

{% hint style="info" %}
You should be able to see the published smart contract details, including the publisher's address, contract address, Views created from the smart contract, functions, and contract details (i.e., ABI) as shown here:

![](https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2F9GzmOy9fXNr7hgqudBwn%2FSample%20Contract%20Details.png?alt=media\&token=98d17627-c801-40ba-9f75-40f9f610b3bc)
{% endhint %}

## How to List a Smart Contract

If the smart contract is not listed, you'll need to list it. There are three methods for listing contracts on Analog Watch: Watch Portal, Watch SDK, and GraphQL IDE.&#x20;

Depending on your choice, here’s how you’ll proceed:

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="Watch Portal" %}

1. Click on the “Smart Contract” link at the top right of the screen. You’ll be presented with a screen similar to this:

   <figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FdgnmB2iEKVq4L3kY59CM%2FListing%20Smart%20Contracts.png?alt=media&amp;token=2a3aee0b-4610-431e-a189-8610c8d63d7a" alt=""><figcaption><p>How to list a Smart Contract on the Watch Portal</p></figcaption></figure>

2. Enter the Smart Contract details, including the contract address, identifier (i.e., unique name for your contract), and instance (e.g., testnet or mainnet).

3. Next, select the chain where the Smart Contract is currently deployed and click “**Add Functions**.”&#x20;

{% hint style="info" %}
You should be able to see a list of available functions for the smart contract. Select the function(s) you want to publish and click the “**List**” button to list the contract. However, in case the protocol doesn’t recognize the contract address, you’ll be able to see a screen similar to this one:

![](https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2Fg0uz9H0yIPzZ9ytqQAoi%2FSmart%20Contract%20Error--ABI%20code.png?alt=media\&token=e4358f5f-bf42-48f2-9456-f4ee2b20138d)

In this case, paste the ABI of your smart contract in the text area and click the “***Next***” button to list it. If the smart contract cannot be found despite providing the ABI, verify the address again, ensure you’ve provided the correct ABI on the next screen, and click the "***Next***" button.&#x20;

If the contract has already been listed, you'll be presented with a screen similar to this:

<img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2Fri1a72itye0iGyygoz9d%2FAlready%20Listed%20Contract.png?alt=media&amp;token=79518e42-d42d-48ba-a265-df76b60483af" alt="" data-size="original">

Click the "***View Smart Contract***" link to inspect the published functions. To add functions that are not yet published:

1. Click on the "***Add Functions***" link.
2. Select the function(s) you would like to add.
3. Click on the "***List***" button. &#x20;

Check out “[Best Practices for Smart Contract Listing](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/best-practices-for-smart-contract-listing)” to learn more.
{% endhint %}

4. Wait for the Smart Contract to be listed on the Watch Portal.&#x20;

**Congratulations! You’ve successfully published your first smart contract using the Watch Portal!**

To unlock the full potential of Analog Watch and discover its extensive features, head over to the following links:

<table data-card-size="large" data-view="cards"><thead><tr><th></th><th></th><th></th><th data-hidden data-card-target data-type="content-ref"></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/deploying-views"><strong>Deploying Views</strong></a></td><td>Jump in and start deploying Views. </td><td></td><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/deploying-views">Deploying Views</a></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/querying-data"><strong>Querying Data</strong></a></td><td>Dive in and start querying data.</td><td></td><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/querying-data">Querying Data</a></td></tr></tbody></table>

{% tab title="Watch SDK" %}

## Prerequisites

Ensure the following components are ready:

1. Installed Watch Client. Check out [Quickstart](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart) for more details.
2. Session key. For more information, check out [Quickstart.](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart)

## Initialization

Initialize the Watch Client as follows:

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from '@analog-labs/timegraph-js';
// Instantiate as follows
const timegraph = new TimegraphClient({
  url: 'http://localhost:4000',
  sessionKey: '0;2Evg4riXZjyK3...'//session key. 
})
```

## List the Smart Contract

Run this code to list your Smart Contract:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```javascript
const smartContract = await timegraph.smartContract.get({ address: 'address', network: ‘network’, identifier: ‘identifier’, chain: ‘chain’, methods: ‘methods’, abi: ‘abi’, scope: ‘scope’, dryRun: ‘boolean’});
```

{% endcode %}

The above contract defines:

1. `address`: Extract the contract with the specified address
2. `network`: Extract contract from specified network
3. `chain`: Look into the specified network chain instead of the mainnet
4. `identifier`: propose identifier for the smart contract functions group
5. `methods`: Look for specified methods only
6. `abi`: Use the specified contract ABI instead of getting the network version
7. `scope`: Merging scope: "global" or "subgraph."
8. `dryRun`: do not merge, just propose mutations
9. `all`: report all functions; it reports only view functions by default.

#### **Congratulations! You’ve successfully published your Smart Contract!**

With just a few simple lines of code, you managed to list a smart contract. Now, it’s time to explore the full potential of the Analog Watch! Here are some quick links to get you started:

{% content-ref url="/pages/XvrGvFwpzi3lQjgCKHRY" %}
[Deploying Views](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/deploying-views)
{% endcontent-ref %}

{% content-ref url="/pages/AuALyhGWfuAwEVPLrEWL" %}
[Querying Data](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/querying-data)
{% endcontent-ref %}
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="GraphQL IDE" %}

## Setup

The simplest way to list your Smart Contract using the GraphQL IDE is to [install the GraphiQL extension](https://chrome.google.com/webstore/detail/graphiql-extension/jhbedfdjpmemmbghfecnaeeiokonjclb). Besides the GraphiQL extension, you can also use any of the available [GraphQL IDEs](https://retool.com/blog/the-best-graphql-ides-and-their-features/). Once installed, launch the extension and point to the [appropriate environment URL](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/deployment-environments).

You’ll first need to create the session key and type it in as an authorization variable in the “Headers” section at the bottom of the middle bar as follows:

```graphql
{
  "Authorization": "<session_key>"
}
```

Check out [Session Key Generator (SKG) ](/documentation/resources/tooling/utilities/session-key-generator)to learn more about creating session keys. Provided the Analog Watch Server is open, and you’ve specified the `session_key`, you should see the following interface:

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FnO2YIx7JdLuPZ7AvQR7N%2FDocumentation%20Explorer.png?alt=media&amp;token=ddb44c8a-c7ee-4f0e-8c2d-86fefa3038b0" alt=""><figcaption><p>GraphiQL UI</p></figcaption></figure>

The GraphiQL interface has three parts:

* The left sidebar contains buttons for opening plugin panes and changing settings.
* The middle bar is where you define mutations and queries.
* The right sidebar displays the results of your mutations and queries.

The left sidebar contains two other sections: the top, which provides buttons for the documentation explorer and history, and the bottom section, which includes buttons for re-fetching the GraphQL schema and short keys dialog.

In the middle panel, specify the authorization interface by typing:

```graphql
query{
  userInfo {
    wallet
    userId
    userRole
    subgraph
  }
}
```

Next, click on “*Headers*” at the bottom of the middle panel and type in the session key you generated earlier as follows:

```graphql
{
  “Authorization”: “<session_key>”
}
```

For example, if the session key generated is:&#x20;

`0;2DjuERvt46Ng38XajMCTjGv9N9BLCYP1WEmTK1G9u9gy;0;*;;y79jcXb4hY3PxbKhSJY2jDo2q9HTXQ3ptKqNpZ3xMGX9NeVxZ7vrKAYMhwXmoWJHHgGQLigTEnrz1tCENPASYUT`, type this in the “Headers” section:

```graphql
{
  "Authorization": "0;2DjuERvt46Ng38XajMCTjGv9N9BLCYP1WEmTK1G9u9gy;0;*;;y79jcXb4hY3PxbKhSJY2jDo2q9HTXQ3ptKqNpZ3xMGX9NeVxZ7vrKAYMhwXmoWJHHgGQLigTEnrz1tCENPASYUT "
}
```

If you’re unsure about a particular action, click on the “***Docs Explorer***” in the upper left-hand section of the IDE, which opens the IDE’s docs.

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2F3LYyFWu90ULtFRaOwvYj%2FDocumentation%20Explorer.png?alt=media&amp;token=552cad0c-5fa4-44d8-8069-ba09488b834b" alt=""><figcaption><p>GraphiQL Docs</p></figcaption></figure>

Now click the “Execute” button or press *Ctrl+ Enter*, or *Cmd + Enter* to run the query. Here’s what you should see on the right sidebar:

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FSMlFAL1rNK8wc9pCFLz9%2FGraphiQL%20Output%20Window.png?alt=media&amp;token=8b46687a-d867-43aa-a454-9484895472d7" alt=""><figcaption><p>GraphiQL Output Window</p></figcaption></figure>

Now that you understand how to use the GraphQL IDE, let's dive right in and list the smart contract.

{% hint style="success" %}
GraphQL provides only two types of operations: ***mutations*** to modify the server-side data and ***queries*** to fetch data. Consider queries as GET calls, while mutations are state-changing methods like DELETE, PUT, PATCH, etc., in REST APIs. You’ll use mutations to publish smart contracts and views, while queries will help you fetch the data from these artifacts.
{% endhint %}

## How to list a smart contract with the GraphQL IDE

You’ll first need to define the mutation that creates the contract. Navigate to the middle panel and run this code:

```graphql
mutation({
contract(
address: String!
network: String!
identifier: String!
chain: String
methods: [String!]
abi: String
scope: String
dryRun: Boolean
all: Boolean
): 
}
```

The above contract defines:

1. `address`: Extract the contract with the specified address
2. `network`: Extract contract from specified network
3. `chain`: Look into the specified network chain instead of the mainnet
4. `identifier`: propose identifier for the smart contract functions group
5. `methods`: Look for specified methods only
6. `abi`: Use the specified contract ABI instead of getting the network version
7. `scope`: Merging scope: "global" or "subgraph."
8. `dryRun`: do not merge, just propose mutations
9. `all`: report all functions; it reports only view functions by default.
   {% endtab %}
   {% endtabs %}

## Additional Resources

{% endcontent-ref %}

# Deploying Views

Learn how to deploy Views with the Watch Portal.

In this section, we'll explore how to deploy a View (as well as proceed to [query the View](https://docs.analog.one/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/querying-data) afterward) by using an Aggregated Data Feed use case as an example. You can also explore other use cases that demonstrate Analog Watch's true potential [here](https://www.analog.one/?section=usecases).

## Problem Overview

Decentralized Finance (DeFi) enables billions of dollars worth of trades across various blockchains. Because many DeFi activities take place on Decentralized Exchanges (DEXs), a colossal volume of data regarding liquidity, token swaps, staking, and more is stored on-chain.

As such, DEXs can provide valuable financial market data through on-chain price feeds, such as exchange rates for cryptocurrencies, commodities, indices, stablecoins, and other essential financial datasets. However, accurate price discovery for a particular asset within the entire crypto space remains an issue because each DEX is typically hosted on a different blockchain, each having a different set of network validators to match orders and perform cross-chain swaps.

Such an issue leads to discrepancies in asset prices across different DEXs, making it difficult for users to trade effectively and make sound investment decisions in the crypto market.

## What We’re Building <a href="#what-were-building" id="what-were-building"></a>

The application we’re building is a simple price feed aggregator. It collects and processes data from two smart contracts: `Uniswap` for the `WETH/USDC` pair and `Curve.Fi` for the `USDT/WBTC/WETH` pool. Here are the addresses for these smart contracts:

* Uniswap: `0x88e6A0c2dDD26FEEb64F039a2c41296FcB3f5640`
* Curve.Fi: `0xD51a44d3FaE010294C616388b506AcdA1bfAAE46`

For more details on how you can list these smart contracts, check out [Listing Smart Contracts](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts).

The price feed aggregator should be able to average these price data and present a mean value of the prices served by Uniswap and Curve (against `USDC` and `USDT`).&#x20;

## Next Steps

We'll deploy this View in three ways: Watch Portal, Watch SDK, and GraphQL IDE. Depending on your selection, here’s how to proceed:

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="Watch Portal" %}
Follow these steps to get started with deploying your Views:

### **Step 1: Check to ensure the relevant Smart Contract(s) and functions are published**

First, ensure the smart contract(s) you intend to use are listed. Check out [Listing Smart Contracts](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts) to learn more.&#x20;

{% hint style="info" %}
In this example, we'll ensure that the two smart contracts we want to use are listed (i.e., `0x88e6A0c2dDD26FEEb64F039a2c41296FcB3f5640` for Uniswap (`WETH/USDC)` and `0xD51a44d3FaE010294C616388b506AcdA1bfAAE46` for CurveFi(`USDT/WBTC/WETH`).&#x20;

Additionally, you'll need to ensure that the relevant functions are also listed. For example, `slot0()`, which stores many values we're interested in the Uniswap smart contract, and `get_dy()`, `get_virtual_price ()` for the CurveFi.&#x20;
{% endhint %}

### **Step 2: Create a View Definition**

After checking that the relevant smart contract(s) are listed, you’ll need to create a View Definition (i.e., a list of smart contracts and their relevant function(s) from which to collect and index the View data). Follow these steps to create a View Definition:

1. Launch the Watch Portal( <https://watch.testnet.analog.one/>) and connect your wallet. &#x20;
2. Now, locate the "*View Builder*" link at the top center of the screen and click on it.  This should open a UI with three panels, as shown below:

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FItTDPzTcf9dFwIwdEZ8S%2FView%20Builder%20Dashboard.png?alt=media&amp;token=b5874419-818a-4f9a-8537-1c6b50c4d58b" alt=""><figcaption><p>How to create a View using Watch Portal</p></figcaption></figure>

The left panel ("Smart Contracts") displays a list of deployed Smart Contracts. The middle panel (“Your Query”) shows the list of the functions and parameters you want the View to index from the selected smart contract. The right panel ("Console") has two tabs: *Definition*, which autogenerates the SQL statements required to fetch and present the contract data, and *Test Run*, which you can use to test drive the View.&#x20;

2. From the left panel (i.e., “Smart Contracts”), select the smart contract(s) you want to index and their relevant functions.&#x20;

{% hint style="info" %}
In this case, we'll select two contracts:&#x20;

* `WETH/USDC`: This collection retrieves and indexes data from the `Uniswap` smart contract on Ethereum. This contract has one basic function: `slot0()`, which stores a list of numbers we're interested in. &#x20;
* `USDT/WBTC/WETH`. This collection retrieves and indexes data from the `Curve.Fi` smart contract. We'll select two functions from this contract (i.e., `get_dy()`, `get_virtual_price ()`)`.`&#x20;
  {% endhint %}

### **Step 3: Test and deploy the query**

Follow these steps to deploy your View:

1. Verify that the autogenerated SQL statements in the Definition tab of the Console match the particular requirements for your use case, then click the “Test Query” button.
2. Now, click on the “Test Query” to proceed. If the query runs successfully, it should take you to the “Test Run” tab. However, if it fails, you must fix the errors in the “Your Query” section. You could even write your own SQL and execute it directly in the Console by clicking the “Test Run” tab.
3. If satisfied with the query results, click “Deploy View” to proceed. Otherwise, modify the query in the "Your Query" section.&#x20;
4. In the next screen, enter the View's name (you can use the identifier), type tags, and a description. Then, click the 'Next' Button.&#x20;
5. Now, select the use cases where your View will be utilized. For use cases that are not listed, click the “Add new use cases” link and type in the name of the use case. Next, click the "Save" button to add the use case to the Analog Watch.&#x20;
6. Click the "Complete" button to deploy your View.

#### **Congratulations! You’ve successfully published your View on the Portal!**

With just a few simple lines of code, you managed to deploy a View. Now, it’s time to explore the full potential of the Analog Watch! Here are some quick links to get you started:

* Head over to the [Funding Views](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/funding-views) page to learn more on how to fund your View. Then, users can start querying it, and you'll earn rewards in the form of query fees.&#x20;
* Visit the [Querying Data](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/querying-data) page to learn more about how to query the View data if the View is funded.&#x20;
  {% endtab %}

{% tab title="Watch SDK" %}

### Prerequisites

Ensure you have the following components ready:

1. Installed the Watch SDK. Check out [Quickstart](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart) to learn more.&#x20;
2. Generated the session key. See [Quickstart](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart) for more details.&#x20;

### How to Deploy the View

Recall that we're still working on our [Aggregated Data Feeds](#problem-overview) use case, which requires indexing data from two contracts: `Uniswap` and `Curve.Fi`.  Follow these two basic steps to deploy your View with the Watch SDK:

#### **Step 1: Define the JSON specification**&#x20;

To deploy the View, you must first define their JSON specifications describing the data you want to index (i.e.,  smart contracts and relevant functions). The important entities to define in the JSON specification include the following:

1. Name of the network/blockchain.
2. Smart contract address.
3. Contract ABI.
4. A list of function(s) that the collection.
5. The inputs that are fed to the function(s) and the output the function generates. Check out [Supported Data Types](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/supported-data-types) to learn more.&#x20;

#### **Step 2: Publish the View**

Next, we will proceed to deploy the `WETH/USDT` View  by running the code below:

{% code overflow="wrap" lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from "@analog-labs/timegraph-js";
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: "<URL>", // A url to Watch GraphQL instance.  
sessionKey: "<SSK>", // Session key created by user wallet using WASM SDK
});
const response = timeGraphClient.view.create({ view: "< view-specification>" }); 
```

{% endcode %}

Response

```json
"response":{
   "merge":{
      "status":"complete",
      ...all other created view fields
   }
}
```

{% tab title="GraphQL IDE" %}

### Setup

The simplest way to deploy a View is to [install the GraphiQL extension](https://chrome.google.com/webstore/detail/graphiql-extension/jhbedfdjpmemmbghfecnaeeiokonjclb) for the Chrome browser. Besides the GraphiQL extension. you can also use any of the available [GraphQL IDEs](https://retool.com/blog/the-best-graphql-ides-and-their-features/). Once installed, launch the extension and point its URL to the [appropriate environment.](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/deployment-environments)

You’ll first need to create the session key and type it in as an authorization variable in the “Headers” section at the bottom of the middle bar as follows:

```graphql
{
  "Authorization": "<session_key>"
}
```

Check out [Session Key Generator (SKG)](/documentation/resources/tooling/utilities/session-key-generator) to learn more about creating session keys. Provided the Analog Watch Server is open, and you’ve specified the `session_key`, you should see the following interface:

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FnO2YIx7JdLuPZ7AvQR7N%2FDocumentation%20Explorer.png?alt=media&amp;token=ddb44c8a-c7ee-4f0e-8c2d-86fefa3038b0" alt=""><figcaption><p>GraphiQL UI</p></figcaption></figure>

The GraphiQL interface has three parts:

* The left sidebar contains buttons for opening plugin panes and changing settings.
* The middle bar is where you define mutations and queries.
* The right sidebar displays the results of your mutations and queries.

The left sidebar contains two other sections: the top, which provides buttons for the documentation explorer and history, and the bottom section, which includes buttons for re-fetching the GraphQL schema and short keys dialog.

In the middle panel, specify the authorization interface by typing:

```graphql
query{
  userInfo {
    wallet
    userId
    userRole
    subgraph
  }
}
```

Next, click on “*Headers*” at the bottom of the middle panel and type in the session key you generated earlier as follows:

```graphql
{
  “Authorization”: “<session_key>”
}
```

For example, if the session key generated is: `0;2DjuERvt46Ng38XajMCTjGv9N9BLCYP1WEmTK1G9u9gy;0;*;;y79jcXb4hY3PxbKhSJY2jDo2q9HTXQ3ptKqNpZ3xMGX9NeVxZ7vrKAYMhwXmoWJHHgGQLigTEnrz1tCENPASYUT`, type this in the “Headers” section:

```graphql
{
  "Authorization": "0;2DjuERvt46Ng38XajMCTjGv9N9BLCYP1WEmTK1G9u9gy;0;*;;y79jcXb4hY3PxbKhSJY2jDo2q9HTXQ3ptKqNpZ3xMGX9NeVxZ7vrKAYMhwXmoWJHHgGQLigTEnrz1tCENPASYUT "
}
```

If you’re unsure about a particular action, click on the “Documentation Explorer” in the upper left-hand section of the IDE, which opens the IDE’s docs.

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2F3LYyFWu90ULtFRaOwvYj%2FDocumentation%20Explorer.png?alt=media&amp;token=552cad0c-5fa4-44d8-8069-ba09488b834b" alt=""><figcaption><p>GraphiQL Docs</p></figcaption></figure>

Now click the “Execute” button or press Ctrl+ Enter, or Cmd + Enter to run the query. Here’s what you should see on the right sidebar:

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FSMlFAL1rNK8wc9pCFLz9%2FGraphiQL%20Output%20Window.png?alt=media&amp;token=8b46687a-d867-43aa-a454-9484895472d7" alt=""><figcaption><p>GraphiQL Output Window</p></figcaption></figure>

Now that you understand how to use the GraphQL IDE, let's deploy the View.&#x20;

{% hint style="info" %}
GraphQL provides only two types of operations: ***mutations*** to modify the server-side data and ***queries*** to fetch data. Consider queries as GET calls, while mutations are state-changing methods like DELETE, PUT, PATCH, etc., in REST APIs. You’ll use mutations to publish Views, while queries will help you fetch the data from these artifacts.
{% endhint %}

### How to Create Views with the GraphQL IDE

You’ll first need to define the function interface to create a View. Navigate to the middle panel and run this:

```graphql
mutation($functions:[FunctionSpec!]){
  merge(functions:$functions){
    status
    functions{
      name
      function{
        hashId
        contract{
          address
        }
      }
    }
  }
}
```

The above functions interface defines:

* Merging schema, which consolidates all the type definitions from different functions into a single executable schema.
* The name of the function(s) within the smart contract you’d like to use.
* The contract name and address.

Using the above function interface, type the code below in the “Variables” section to define the functions:

```graphql
{
“functions”: [
    {
      “name”: “UniSwap:USDC_getAmountsOut”,
      “inputs”: [
        {“input”: “number_as_uint256”},
        {
          “array”: [
            {
              “constant”: {
                “value”: “A0b86991c6218b36c1d19D4a2e9Eb0cE3606eB48”,
                “input”: “hex_address”
              }
            },
            {“input”: “hex_address”}
          ]
        }
      ],
      "outputs": [{ "output": "skip" }, { "output": "uint256_as_number" }],
      “network”: “Ethereum”,
      “contract”: {
        “address”: “7a250d5630B4cF539739dF2C5dAcb4c659F2488D”,
        “abi”: {
          “name”: “getAmountsOut”,
          “stateMutability”: “view”,
          “type”: “function”,
          “inputs”: [
            {
              "internalType": "uint256",
              “name”: “amountIn”,
              “type”: “uint256”
            },
            {
              “internalType”: “address[]”,
              “name”: “path”,
              “type”: “address[]”
            }
          ],
          “outputs”: [
            {
              "internalType": "uint256[]",
              “name”: “amounts”,
              “type”: “uint256[]”
            }
          ]
        }
      }
    }
  ]
}

```

This function configuration defines the following:

* Name of the function (i.e., UniSwap:`USDC_getAmountsOut`).
* Inputs to the function (i.e., `amountIn`, of type `uint256`, and `path`, of type “`address`”).
* Function output (i.e., `amounts` of type `uint256`).
* Contract address and ABI.

When you run this code, you should see a unique hash ID for the function created, as shown below:

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FBTEkmqqbaAnPI5zPQbDo%2FSmart%20contract%20function.png?alt=media&amp;token=6f65a12c-4584-45dc-bd72-90c90b5478f1" alt=""><figcaption><p>Creating a function</p></figcaption></figure>

Let’s now create some Views from the created function. First, run a mutation operation to create the collection interface as follows:&#x20;

```graphql
mutation($views:[ViewSpec!]){
  merge(views:$views){
    status
    views{
      name
      view{
        hashId
        sql
      }
    }
  }
}

```

You can now create a single View (one that connects to a single smart contract) or a complex View (one that queries data from multiple smart contracts) by running the code below:

```graphql
{
"views": [
    {
      "name": "by_name",
      "sql": "select Price, 'USDC/WETH' as Pair from USDC_WETH"
    },
    {
      "name": "simple",
      "sql": "select Price, 'USDC/WETH' as Pair, _cycle, _block from USDC_WETH"
    },
    {
      "name": "complex",
      "sql": "select u.Price as UNI, w.Price as WETH, w._cycle, w._block from USDC_WETH as w join USDC_UNI as u on u._block = w._block"
    }
  ]
}
```

Creating a View generates a hash ID and other details, such as the view name and SQL statement on the Analog Watch. Depending on your use case, you can modify the SQL statement to fetch data from a single collection or multiple Views in the case of a complex view.

In the above case:

* The first view is a single view. It retrieves price and USDC/WETH as a pair from the USDC\_WETH smart contract and stores it as “by\_name” on the Timegraph protocol.&#x20;
* Similarly, the second view is a single view. It fetches the price, USDC\_WETH as pair, \_cycle, and a block number from the USDC\_WETH  smart contract.
* The third view is a complex view that retrieves data from two smart contracts.

#### **Congratulations! You’ve successfully published your View with the GraphQL IDE!**

With just a few simple lines of code, you managed to deploy a View. Now, it’s time to explore the full potential of the Analog Watch! Here are some quick links to get you started:

* Head over to the "[Funding Views](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/funding-views)" page to learn more on how to fund your View. Then, users can start querying it, and you'll earn rewards in the form of query fees.&#x20;
* Visit the "[Querying Data](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/querying-data)" page to learn more about how to query the View data if the View is funded.&#x20;
  {% endtab %}
  {% endtabs %}

# Funding Views

Learn more about funding Views.

Before a View can be indexed, [Data Collectors](/documentation/resources/glossary#data-collectors) need to sponsor/fund it. While any independent Data Collector can sponsor your View, typically, you would want to be among the first sponsors to ensure the View is indexed. There are three methods for funding Views on Analog Watch: Watch Portal, Watch SDK, and GraphQL IDE.&#x20;

Depending on your selection, proceed as follows:&#x20;

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="Watch Portal" %}
Follow these steps to fund a View:

1. Launch the Analog Watch by visiting the [appropriate environment URL ](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/deployment-environments)and connecting your wallet.
2. Head over to the Library section by clicking on the “Library” link. Select the View you want to fund by clicking on it. You should be able to see a screen similar to this:

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FfQfsDnNHfnzyWqYkgyfL%2FHow%20to%20fund%20a%20View.png?alt=media&amp;token=4381aca2-a6cb-4342-8ed9-48946047d9b9" alt=""><figcaption><p>How to fund a View</p></figcaption></figure>

3. Next, locate the “Add Funds” button and click on it.&#x20;
4. Enter the amount of tokens you would like to allocate to the View, then click the “Add Funds” button to fund the View.
5. Wait for the transaction to be finalized on the Timechain.
   {% endtab %}

{% tab title="Watch SDK" %}

## Prerequisites

Ensure the following components are ready:

1. Installed Watch Client. Check out [Quickstart](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart) for more details.
2. Session key. For more information, check out [Quickstart.](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart)
3. Enough tokens in your wallet. For the testnet tokens, check out [Testnet Faucet ](/documentation/analog-network/getting-started/analog-testnet-faucet)for more details.

## Initialization

Initialize the Watch Client as follows:

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from '@analog-labs/timegraph-js';
// Instantiate as follows
const timegraph = new TimegraphClient({
  url: 'http://localhost:4000',
  sessionKey: '0;2Evg4riXZjyK3...'
})
```

## Fund the View

Proceed to fund the View by running this code:

```javascript
const fund  =  await client.tokenomics.sponsorView({
        viewName:"QUANT_FUNCS",
        amount:"2000000000"
    })
```

Running the above code funds a View named "`QUANT_FUNCS`" with `2000000000 TOCK`.&#x20;

{% hint style="info" %}
**Note:**

TOCK is the base unit of the ANLOG token. Check out [Tokenomics](/documentation/resources/tokenomics) to learn more.
{% endhint %}
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="GraphQL IDE" %}

## Prerequisites

Ensure the following components are ready:

1. Session key. For more information, check out [Quickstart.](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart)
2. Enough tokens in your wallet. For the testnet tokens, check out [Network Faucet](/documentation/analog-network/getting-started/analog-testnet-faucet) for more details.
3. GraphQL IDE. Check out [Listing Smart Contracts](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts) for more details.&#x20;

## Fund the View

Follow these steps to fund the View:

1. Launch your browser and point it to the [appropriate environment URL](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/deployment-environments).
2. Navigate to the middle panel of the GraphQL IDE's interface and run a code similar to this one:&#x20;

```graphql
sponsorView(
viewName: String!
amount: String!
): 

```

For example, here is how you would fund a View named "UniswapUSDC" with 200000 TOCK:

```graphql
sponsorView(
viewName: UniswapUSDC
amount: 200000
): 
```

{% hint style="info" %}
TOCK is the base unit of the ANLOG token. Check out "[Tokenomics](/documentation/resources/tokenomics)" to learn more.
{% endhint %}
{% endtab %}
{% endtabs %}

{% hint style="warning" %}
You’ll need enough tokens to [fund your View](#fund-the-view). Check out the [Testnet Faucet ](/documentation/analog-network/getting-started/analog-testnet-faucet)page to learn more about acquiring testnet tokens.
{% endhint %}


# Querying Data

Learn how to query data with the Watch SDK.

Currently, the Watch Portal doesn’t permit direct querying of data from the GraphQL endpoint. You can only use it to explore published smart contracts and Views. To query data directly from a GraphQL endpoint, you can use the Watch SDK or the GraphQL IDE.&#x20;

## How to Query Data with the Watch SDK

### Prerequisites <a href="#prerequisites" id="prerequisites"></a>

Before diving into the Watch SDK, ensure you have completed the following checklist components:

* Install Watch Client. Check out [Watch SDK](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart) for more details.
* Generate a Session key. Check out [Authentification](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/quickstart) to learn more.&#x20;
* Ensure you have the URL of the deployed View. Visit the [appropriate environment URL](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/deployment-environments) to explore a list of published Views and their URLs. We'll use the price feed aggregator View we have explained [here](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/deploying-views) and [funded](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/funding-views) for illustration.&#x20;

### Displaying a List of Published Artifacts <a href="#display-a-list-of-published-artifacts" id="display-a-list-of-published-artifacts"></a>

Depending on which Watch artifact list you would like to display, here's a quick way to do it:

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="Functions" %}
{% code lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from "@analog-labs/timegraph-js";
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: "<URL>", // A url to Watch GraphQL instance.  
sessionKey: "<SSK>", // Session key created by user wallet using WASM SDK
});
const functions = await timeGraphClient.fn.subgraph({
 after:"<after the hashID>",
 before:"<before the hashiD>",
 hash:"<Current HashId>",
 limit:"<number of records>"
 });
```

{% endcode %}
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="Views" %}
{% code lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from "@analog-labs/timegraph-js";
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: "<URL>", // A url to Watch GraphQL instance.  
sessionKey: "<SSK>", // Session key created by user wallet using WASM SDK
});
const queriedData = await timeGraphClient.view.subgraph({
 after:"<after the hashID>",
 before:"<before the hashiD>",
 hash:"<Current HashId>",
 limit:"<number of records>"
});
```

{% endcode %}
{% endtab %}
{% endtabs %}

### Querying Data from Published Views

Here's an example code showcasing how to query data from a published View:

{% code lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from "@analog-labs/timegraph-js";
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: "<URL>", // A url to Watch GraphQL instance.  
sessionKey: "<SSK>", // Session key created by user wallet using WASM SDK
});
// i.e If you're querying other publishers' View, then an alias request will be called in first.
// Optional Alias request to query other publisher view. 
const aliasResponse = await timeGraphClient.alias.add({
name:"<name of the view>",
identifier:"<identifier for subgraph>"
})	
// Request to query the view..... 
const response = await timeGraphClient.view.data(
{ 
hashId:"<hash id of the View>",
fields:["<fields to return>"],
limit:"<no of records required>"
}); 
```

{% endcode %}

**Response**

{% code lineNumbers="true" %}

```json
{
	response: {
		"view": {
            "USDC_WETH" : [
              {
                  "_cycle": "6",
                  "_block": "17087521",
                  "Price": "0.00051192"
                }
          }
}
```

{% endcode %}

### Querying User Info <a href="#query-user-info" id="query-user-info"></a>

In addition to publishing and querying Views, the Watch SDK lets you query user information, including your **registered data**, **API keys**, and **subgraph details**.

#### 1. Registered data

Run this code to fetch your registered data from the Watch SDK:

{% code lineNumbers="true" %}

```javascript
import { TimegraphClient } from "@analog-labs/timegraph-js";
const timeGraphClient = new TimegraphClient({
url: "<URL>", // A url to Watch GraphQL instance.  
sessionKey: "<SSK>", // Session key created by user wallet using WASM SDK
});
const response = await timeGraphClient.user.get();
); 
```

{% endcode %}

**Response**

{% code lineNumbers="true" %}

```json
{
	response: {
			"userInfo" : {
           "userId" : 100,
           "userRole" : "developer",
           "subgraph": 100
				}
}
```

{% endcode %}

```javascript
);
```

```javascript
	limit:"<limit of records >"
});
```
