## How to Query Data with the GraphQL IDE

### Prerequisites

Ensure you have the following components ready:

1. Your Session Key. Check out [Session Key Generator](/documentation/resources/tooling/utilities/session-key-generator) for more details.
2. Your chosen GraphQL IDE. We'll use the GraphiQL IDE in this example. However, you can use other GraphQL IDEs listed [here](https://graphqleditor.com/blog/top-5-graphql-ides-for-2023/).&#x20;
3. Set up the environment for the GraphQL IDE. See [Setup](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts#setup) to learn more.

### Querying Process

Querying data with the GraphQL IDE is a simple two-step process:

1. Launch your browser and point the URL to the [appropriate environment](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/deployment-environments).
2. To query any View, locate the middle panel of the IDE's Editor and type or copy this:

```graphql
query{
    simple(limit:5){
      price
      _cycle
      _block
    }
    
    complex(limit:10){
      uni,
      weth
      _block
      _cycle
    }
  }

```

{% hint style="info" %}
The first statement (i.e., `simple(limit:5){}`) queries a list of five records from a View named "`simple`." Each fetched record has 3 fields: `price`, `cycle`*,* and `block`. On the other hand, the second statement (i.e., `complex(limit:10){}`) fetches 10 records from a View named "`complex`," where each fetched record has 4 fields: `uni`, `weth`, `block`*, and* `_cycle`*.*
{% endhint %}

Besides typical queries, you can also query other details, such as registered data, API keys, and global artifacts with the GraphQL IDE:

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="Registered Details" %}
Run this code to fetch your registered data:

```graphql
query{
  userInfo {
    wallet
    userId
    userRole
    subgraph
  }
}
```

{% endtab %}

{% tab title="API Keys" %}
Run this code to fetch the API keys:

```graphql
query{
  keys {
    key
  }
}

```

{% endtab %}

{% tab title="Subgraph" %}
Subgraph details let you extract functions and Views associated with a given session key. For example, to extract functions, you can run the following code:

```graphql
query{
  subgraph {
    after
    before
    hash
    limit
  }
}

```

{% endtab %}

{% tab title="Global" %}
You can also query other artifacts that have been created by other users as follows:

1. **Contracts**

```graphql
query{
  contracts {
    address
    network
    limit
    before
    after
  }
}

```

2. **Views**

```graphql
query{
  views {
    hash
    limit
    before
    after
  }
}

```

3. **Functions**

```graphql
query{
  functions {
    hash
    limit
    before
    after
  }
}

```

{% endtab %}
{% endtabs %}


# Rewards & Fees

{% hint style="warning" %}
Analog Watch will start as a centralized service but aims to decentralize Web3 data for universal, reliable access. However, the platform’s long-term vision is to use the [Timechain](/documentation/developers/protocol-overview#accountability-layer) to generate cryptographic Watch Proofs for independent data validation. (Refer to the [Watch Tokenomics Paper](https://docs.google.com/document/u/1/d/e/2PACX-1vR6vgYFvmS3kDDmwjuJMG6hcy9g58sUi90nY6vC8sbztaF0pSrVWyURmRClbtqnvA/pub) for more details.) For now, Analog Watch uses the Timechain to secure and track user deposits by:

* Reserving funds in user-specific Timechain accounts upon deposit.
* Enabling secure, user-initiated withdrawals that release reserved funds on the Timechain.
  {% endhint %}

## Role of $ANLOG in the Analog Watch Ecosystem

$ANLOG is the native cryptocurrency of the Analog Network (see [Tokenomics](/documentation/resources/tokenomics) for more details). It's also a utility token within the Analog Watch ecosystem, used to coordinate and reward interactions between users and power transactions.

### **Key Functions for Data Consumers**

1. **Query Payments**: Data Consumers deposit $ANLOG to fulfill data queries. These tokens are distributed to Data Collectors and Designers as compensation.
2. **Account Management via Timechain**:
   * Deposit/withdraw $ANLOG to manage balances.
   * Track real-time balances (deposits vs. withdrawals).
   * Enable automatic query payments when sufficient funds are available.

Query fees are tied to API key usage. For full details on tokenomics, refer to the [Watch Tokenomics Paper.](https://docs.google.com/document/u/1/d/e/2PACX-1vR6vgYFvmS3kDDmwjuJMG6hcy9g58sUi90nY6vC8sbztaF0pSrVWyURmRClbtqnvA/pub)

## Ecosystem Stakeholders

Analog Watch consists of five primary stakeholders:

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="Data Designers" %}
Data Designers are entities that [publish smart contracts](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts) and [Views](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/deploying-views) on the Analog Watch. Anyone with sufficient knowledge about smart contracts can undertake this role on Analog Watch. Data designers earn a portion of query fees for their services. Check out [Watch Tokenomics Paper](https://docs.google.com/document/u/1/d/e/2PACX-1vR6vgYFvmS3kDDmwjuJMG6hcy9g58sUi90nY6vC8sbztaF0pSrVWyURmRClbtqnvA/pub) to learn more.&#x20;
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="Data Collectors" %}
Data Collectors fund the indexing and processing of Views by [funding/sponsoring ](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/funding-views)them. They could be developers, consumers, or community members who signal to Data Providers that Views should be given priority during the indexing process. Data Collectors earn a portion of query fees for their services. Check out [Watch Tokenomics Paper](https://docs.google.com/document/u/1/d/e/2PACX-1vR6vgYFvmS3kDDmwjuJMG6hcy9g58sUi90nY6vC8sbztaF0pSrVWyURmRClbtqnvA/pub) to learn more.
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="Data Consumers" %}
Data Consumers are users or applications of Analog Watch that explore and query Views by paying query fees. Check out [Querying Plans](#querying-plans) and [Watch Tokenomics Paper](https://docs.google.com/document/u/1/d/e/2PACX-1vR6vgYFvmS3kDDmwjuJMG6hcy9g58sUi90nY6vC8sbztaF0pSrVWyURmRClbtqnvA/pub) for more details.&#x20;
{% endtab %}
{% endtabs %}

## Revenue Streams

Data Designers and Data Collectors are compensated for View data through Fees paid by Data Consumers, calculated using the following formula:

$$Fee=Base\_Query\_Fee+View\_Weight*Cadence*Rate$$

Where:

1. $$Fee$$ is a fixed fee that Data Collectors/Data Designers earn from their work.&#x20;
2. $$Base\_Query\_Fee$$ is the base fee denoting a fixed cost for the query service. It covers the basic operational expenses.&#x20;
3. $$View\_Weight$$ is the volume of data queried.
4. $$Cadence$$ is the cadence of indexing. For example, the cadence could be every 10 blocks for an epoch or 24 hours.
5. $$Rate$$ is a configurable factor that allows the recovery of the total cost based on the total volume of data queried so far.&#x20;

{% hint style="success" %}
The generated $$Fee$$ is distributed to Data Collectors/Data Designers on an epoch (i.e., 12-hour period) basis. Initially, all query fees go to the Data Collector. Once profitable, fees are shared between the Data Collector and Data Designer in a 9:1 ratio, respectively. Check out [Watch Tokenomics Paper](https://docs.google.com/document/u/1/d/e/2PACX-1vR6vgYFvmS3kDDmwjuJMG6hcy9g58sUi90nY6vC8sbztaF0pSrVWyURmRClbtqnvA/pub) for more details.&#x20;
{% endhint %}

## Querying Plans

Two query plans are available for Views on the Analog Watch:

* **Free Plan**: This is the initial plan for any Data Consumer and includes `100 free queries` with full access to the Watch Portal and Watch SDK features. This plan is designed for projects that want to test out Analog Watch before scaling their operations.
* **Premium Plan**: This plan includes all features of the Free Plan, but requires payment for queries exceeding `100`, as detailed in[ Revenue Streams](#revenue-streams).

## Key Parameters & Defaults

| **Parameter**               | **Default Value** | **Description**                                                             |
| --------------------------- | ----------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `EXISTENTIAL_BALANCE`       | 1,000,000,000     | Minimum balance required for users to submit queries.                       |
| `QUERY_BASE_FEE`            | 20                | Base fee per query (adjusted dynamically).                                  |
| `VIEW_CREATION_FEE`         | 100,000,000       | Cost to create a new View                                                   |
| `CONTRACT_CREATION_FEE`     | 10,000,000        | Cost to create a new contract.                                              |
| `ETHEREUM_WEIGHT`           | 3                 | Weight for Ethereum network fee calculations (higher = more expensive).     |
| `UNSYNCED_BALANCE_THRESHOL` | -100,000,000      | Threshold to flag accounts with unsynced balances (negative value in code). |
| `FREE_TRIAL_TIMES`          | 100               | Number of free queries allowed per user before fees apply.                  |
| `UPDATE_INTERVAL`           | 60 seconds        | Parameter refresh rate.                                                     |
| `SETTLEMENT_INTERVAL`       | 2 hours           | Settlement window for balances.                                             |


# Supported Data Types

Analog Watch data types are standard, with a few differences based on the onboarded blockchains, as outlined in this section.

Each value manipulated by the collection you deploy has a data type. The data type associates a fixed set of properties for that value. These properties cause the collection to treat values of one type differently from values of another. These types are standard, with few differences based on the onboarded blockchain. The table below provides a summary of the data types that Analog Watch supports:

| Input Types                                                                                                                                 | Output Types                                                                                                                                | Unsupported Types                   |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------- |
| **Unsigned integers** (`uint`): This includes `uint`, `uint256`, `uint160`, `uint128`, `uint64`, `uint32`, `uint24`, `uint16`, and `uint8`. | **Unsigned integers** (`uint`): This includes `uint`, `uint256`, `uint160`, `uint128`, `uint64`, `uint32`, `uint24`, `uint16`, and `uint8`. | Fixed-size arrays                   |
| **Signed integers**(`int`): This includes: `int`, `int256`,          `int160`, `int128`, `int64`, `int32`, `int24`, `int16`, and `int8`.    | **Signed integers**(`int`): This includes: `int`, `int256`,          `int160`, `int128`, `int64`, `int32`, `int24`, `int16`, and `int8`.    | <p>Dynamic-size arrays</p><p> </p>  |
| <p><code>string</code> </p><p> </p>                                                                                                         | <p><code>string</code> </p><p> </p>                                                                                                         | <p>Array members</p><p> </p>        |
| <p><code>boolean/bool</code></p><p> </p>                                                                                                    | <p><code>boolean/bool</code></p><p> </p>                                                                                                    | <p>Byte arrays</p><p> </p>          |
| `address`                                                                                                                                   | `address`                                                                                                                                   | <p>String arrays</p><p> </p>        |
| `bytes`                                                                                                                                     |                                                                                                                                             | <p><code>structs</code></p><p> </p> |
|                                                                                                                                             |                                                                                                                                             | `mapping`                           |
|                                                                                                                                             |                                                                                                                                             | <p><code>uint112</code></p><p> </p> |


# Best Practices for Smart Contract Listing

Guidelines for effectively listing smart contracts on Analog Watch.

The smart contract you list on the Analog Watch outlines the functions and data types available for other platform users to generate Views, bridging the gap between Analog Watch and [Data Designers](/documentation/resources/glossary#data-designers)/[Data Consumers](/documentation/resources/glossary#data-consumers). As such, the listed contract must be clear, easy to hand off, and free from assumptions and confusion.

{% hint style="warning" %}
Remember, once a smart contract is listed, it cannot be relisted. That is why it is important to adhere to best practices, as you won’t have another opportunity to rebuild your contract.
{% endhint %}

Here are a few best practices you can follow when listing smart contracts on the Analog Watch:

## Choose meaningful names for your identifiers

When choosing names for the smart contract identifier, it’s important to choose meaningful and relevant names. An identifier needs to state the purpose of the smart contract, answering questions such as:

* Why do users need that smart contract?
* What is this smart contract doing or storing?

For example, let’s assume you’re listing Uniswap’s price feed contract on the Ethereum blockchain that lists `ETH/USDC` tokens.

**Good example:**

* `UniswapETHUSDC`
* `EthereumUniswapETHUSDC`
* `UniswapETHUSDC`

**Bad example:**

* `Ethereum_2018`
* `ANGEL`
* `ETH_USDC_2016`

Please note that you can't use symbols in your smart contract name.

## Use pronounceable names for your identifiers.

Names that are easy to pronounce allow other users (i.e., [Data Designers](/documentation/resources/glossary#data-designers) and [Data Consumers)](/documentation/resources/glossary#data-consumers) to grasp the functionality of your smart contract within the network.

**Good example:**

* `PancakeSwapWETHUSDT`
* `CurveFiUSDTBTCWETH`

**Bad example:**

* `AT1_4046`
* `CZKO_2022`

## Snake case vs. Camel case

Two generally accepted conventions exist for creating variable or function names: [**snake case**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snake_case#), which uses underscores to separate words in an identifier (e.g., `uniswap_eth_usdc`),  and [**camel case**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camel_case), which uses capital letters to indicate word boundaries (e.g., `uniswapEthUsdc)`. The convention you opt to use depends on your personal preference and the readability of your code.&#x20;

## Always provide a brief description of your smart contract.

Providing a brief description of your smart contract is essential because it helps other users, such as Data Designers and even yourself, understand the smart contract's purpose and structure. It makes your listed smart contract more readable. For example, our `UniswapETHUSDC` would be described simply as:

> Uniswap ETH/USDC token pair on Ethereum.

## Always use tags to enhance the categorization of your listed smart contracts.

Adding a tag to a smart contract is an excellent way to enable Analog Watch to organize your smart contract. It’s also a good way to lead other users (i.e., [Data Designers ](/documentation/resources/glossary#data-designers)and [Data Consumers)](/documentation/resources/glossary#data-consumers) to your smart contract whenever they search for similar contracts. &#x20;


# Best Practices for View Deployment

Guidelines for effectively deploying Views on Analog Watch.

A [View](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/overview/architecture#what-is-a-view) operates on the Analog Watch like a customizable API, allowing users to query specific data from a listed smart contract via a GraphQL endpoint. Before you build a View, it’s important to be familiar with some essential tips about Views.

{% hint style="danger" %}

* As is the case with smart contracts, you cannot delete a View once it’s deployed on the Analog Watch.
* Once the View Definition is deployed, it cannot be altered on Analog Watch.
* To enable faster and easier data queries from a deployed View (leading to greater rewards), you must ensure it meets specific criteria.
  {% endhint %}

The best way to start deploying Views effectively is to follow the guidelines outlined on the “[Best Practices for Listing Smart Contracts](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/best-practices-for-smart-contract-listing)” page.&#x20;

{% content-ref url="/pages/eARIi74xW5yG3ychwNWd" %}
[Best Practices for Smart Contract Listing](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/best-practices-for-smart-contract-listing)
{% endcontent-ref %}

In addition to these guidelines, here are other best practices you need to follow when deploying Views on the Analog Watch:

## 1. Outline the aims and scope of your View.

Before creating a View, it’s crucial to clearly understand what you want to accomplish. This could involve filtering, aggregating, or performing calculations on the indexed data. Additionally, you need to define the scope of your View by deciding whether the View would operate on a single contract on a single chain, multiple contracts on a single chain, or multiple contracts across multiple chains.

Defining a clear purpose and scope for your View enables you to identify the relevant functions within the smart contract(s) that will assist you in reaching your goal.

## 2. Ensure the View Definition is defined properly.

The [View Definition ](/documentation/resources/glossary#view-definition)specifies all the data required to index and query a listed View on the Analog Watch. It serves as an entry point to your View and is deployed to the Analog Watch, as well as hashed to generate a hash ID. This ID can then be referenced to retrieve the View on the Analog Watch.

Here are some tips to consider when defining a [View Definition](/documentation/resources/glossary#view-definition):

* Always ensure that the appropriate smart contract is listed. Check to ensure that the listed smart contract is the right one by verifying its address and contract ABI.&#x20;
* Ensure that the contract’s relevant function(s) you want to use for your View are published. Refer to this page to learn more about adding function(s) to a listed smart contract.
* Ensure you’ve entered the input function arguments if required for your View. You can test the query by clicking the “Test Query” command in the Console section to determine if your function(s) have errors. If any function(s) has a mistake, you’ll be prompted to review and correct the errors.

## Avoid ambiguity in field names and measures.

Expose aggregatable numeric fields in a View with names distinct from measures to avoid confusion. You could hide these fields in the [View Definition ](/documentation/resources/glossary#view-definition)and create explicit measures. For example, if you want to display the “`AVERAGE of ETH/USDT token across two DEXs`,” then you cannot use a name such as “`Mean Price`” as a column name if any of the contracts on either DEX has this name as its column. &#x20;

Using weird names, such as “Average of the token prices” for a measure, isn’t a good solution either. The best practice would be to import the field into the View Definition with some naming convention, then hide it and expose it as a measure.

## Check to ensure you’ve removed useless columns.

Even if you're unsure which fields will be needed in your View Definition, aim to expose only the essential ones. For example, you could leave out high-cardinality fields, such as timestamps, that may only be required for technical reasons.&#x20;

Reducing the number of exposed columns reduces the amount of data that Analog Watch indexes and handles, which is essential in enhancing the View’s performance.

{% hint style="info" %}
Note that each View you create will have two autogenerated `uint256` fields: `_clock` and `_index`Analog Watch in the backend uses these fields to identify each indexed record, and as such, you can leave them as they are. However, you need to ensure that other fields are unique, especially when combining data from multiple contracts/chains.
{% endhint %}


# Event Data Support

Analog Watch makes it easy to work with event data. The [GraphQL IDE](https://timegraph.testnet.analog.one/) lets you specify the desired events, such as `transfers`, `withdrawals`, `approvals`, `swaps`, etc. to be retrieved from smart contracts. It’s noteworthy that event data support is currently available on *Ethereum*, *Polygon*, and *Astar* blockchains, with additional networks planned for future integration.

## How It Works

Indexing an event from your smart contract is as simple as submitting a contract address and specifying the event name in the [GraphQL IDE](https://timegraph.testnet.analog.one/). In this example, we’ll guide you through querying the`transfer` event for a smart contract deployed on Polygon.&#x20;

### **Step 1:** Navigate to the [**PolygonScan Explorer**](https://polygonscan.com/tokens)

1. Select the contract from which you want to retrieve event data.
2. Copy the contract address and check if it's already listed in our [GraphQL IDE](https://timegraph.testnet.analog.one/). If it is not listed, [add the contract first.](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts)
3. You can also check if the contract is listed using the [Watch UI](https://watch.testnet.analog.one/)

### **Step 2: Choose the event**

1. Choose the event you want to query from the contract, such as `Transfer`, `Withdrawal`, `Approval`, `Swap`, etc. For this example, we'll use the `Transfer` event.

### **Step 3: Configure Event Details in the GraphQL IDE**

1. Open the [GraphQL IDE](https://timegraph.testnet.analog.one/).
2. Use the **`merge`** mutation to add the event to your View.
3. Configure functions:

* **`Name:`** `xPolygon_c213_Transfer` → (include the network and address in the name)
* **`Network:`** "`Polygon`" → (specify the network)
* **`Description:`** "Transfer event emitted for a DEX smart contract on the Polygon network."
* **`Outputs:`**&#x43;onfigure the `outputs`. Here's the sample `outputs`:

<details>

<summary>Outputs</summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```json
{ value: "data", name: "from" }
{ value: "data", name: "to" }
{ value: "data", name: "value" }
```

{% endcode %}

</details>

* **`Input:`** → (Configure inputs based on the event)

### **Step 4: Configure Contract Details in the GraphQL IDE**

* **`Address:`** `0xc2132D05D31c914a87C6611C10748AEb04B58e8F` →(contract address from the Polygon Explorer)
* **`ABI:`** name: "`Transfer`", type: "`event`"
* **`Inputs:`** Configure your `inputs` as shown here:

<details>

<summary>Inputs</summary>

```json
[{ type: "address", indexed: true },
 { type: "address", indexed: true },
 { type: "uint256", indexed: false }]
```

</details>

* **`Outputs:`** \[]

### **Step 5:** Create a View for the Event

Create a View (custom query) for the event. You can combine multiple events or smart contract functions if needed. For this example, we'll create a View with the `Transfer` event.

* **`name:`** `xPolygon_c213_Transfer_Event` → (include the address and network in the name)
* **`sql:`** `SELECT * FROM 'xPolygon_c213_Transfer`'
* **`description:`** Event view for Transfer events on a DEX smart contract on the Polygon network.

Here’s a mutation request example for a View:

<details>

<summary>Mutation Request Example</summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```graphql
mutation EventCreation {
  merge(
    functions: [
      {
        name: "xPolygon_c213_Transfer"
        network: "Polygon"
        description: "Transfer event emitted for DEX smart contract on Polygon network."
        outputs: [
          { value: "data", name: "from" }
          { value: "data", name: "to" }
          { value: "data", name: "value" }
        ]
        inputs: []
        contract: {
          address: "0xc2132D05D31c914a87C6611C10748AEb04B58e8F"
          abi: {
            name: "Transfer"
            type: "event"
            inputs: [
              { type: "address", indexed: true }
              { type: "address", indexed: true }
              { type: "uint256", indexed: false }
            ]
            outputs: []
          }
        }
      }
    ]
    views: [
      {
        name: "xPolygon_c213_Transfer_Event"
        sql: "SELECT * FROM '{xPolygon_c213_Transfer}'"
        description: Event view for Transfers that occur on DEXapp for on Polygon network.”
      }
    ]
  ) {
    status
    views {
      status
      error
      name
      header
      view {
        name
        identifier
        hashId
        status {
          errors {
            at
          }
          updates {
            at
          }
        }
        creator
      }
    }
    functions {
      function {
        hashId
        name
        contract {
          abi {
            name
            outputs {
              type
              indexed
              __typename
            }
            inputs {
              type
              indexed
              __typename
            }
            anonymous
          }
          address
        }
        description
        identifier
      }
    }
  }
}
```

{% endcode %}

</details>

### Step 6: Session Key Authorization

Pass the authorization token and session key (SSK) in the header of your request like this:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```graphql
{ "Authorization": Use your session key}
```

{% endcode %}

### Step 7: Sponsor the event View

1. Use the View name from the previous step to locate the View to sponsor.
2. Execute a mutation request to sponsor the View. Here’s an example:

<details>

<summary>Sponsor View Mutation</summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```graphql
mutation sponsorView {
  sponsorView(viewName: "xPloygon_7ceB_Transfer_Event", amount: "2000000000") {
    status
    error
    amount
    view {
      sql
      hashId
      name
      identifier
      description
      references {
        hashId
      }
      creator
      viewId
    }
  }
}
```

{% endcode %}

</details>

{% hint style="info" %}
Alternatively, you can sponsor Views directly by searching their name on the [Watch Portal](https://watch.testnet.analog.one/). Simply locate the view you want on the Watch Portal and [sponsor](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/funding-views) it.&#x20;
{% endhint %}

### Step 8: Query the view

Finally, query the View to retrieve the data by executing the following request:

<details>

<summary>Query View</summary>

```graphql
query global {
  global {
    query(view: "xPloygon_7ceB_Transfer_Event") {
      header
      data
      schema
    }
  }
}

```

</details>


# How to Use Mobile Wallets with Analog Watch

The majority of the mobile wallets are third-party solutions for the Substrate ecosystem. This page provides general instructions for setting up and using mobile wallets with Analog Watch.

{% hint style="info" %}

## Before Getting Started&#x20;

Most mobile wallets are available for both Android and iOS devices. You can install these applications directly from the Google Play Store for Android and App Store for iOS-based wallets, which guarantees that you’re receiving the correct software. Visit [Mobile Wallets ](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/how-to-use-mobile-wallets-with-analog-watch)to find the mobile wallets that support Analog Watch.
{% endhint %}

## Getting Started

Follow these steps to use mobile wallets with Analog Watch:

1. **Install the wallet**. Launch the Google Play Store or App Store and install the wallet on your device. Visit [Mobile Wallets](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/how-to-use-mobile-wallets-with-analog-watch) to learn more about the mobile wallets that support Analog Watch. &#x20;
2. **Create or import the wallet**. If this is the first time you’re installing the wallet, open the application and create a new wallet. You’ll need to create a master password and store the generated seed phrase in a safe location. You can also import the wallet if you’ve used the application before.
3. **Launch the wallet app and Analog Watch**. Open the wallet app. Next, open your browser and point it to <https://watch.testnet.analog.one/>.
4. **Connect the wallet. Locate the “Connect Wallet” link on the Analog Watch page and tap on it. Next, tap on the account you want to connect to. Then, enter your master password to connect the account.**
5. **Generate the API keys**. Upon successfully connecting the wallet, locate the “***My Profile”*** link and tap on it. Under the API Keys tab, tap “***Create New API Key***.” Type in the name of the project you want to associate the API keys with and tap the “***Next***” button. You’ll need to sign off the message for the API keys to be generated. Acknowledge that you’ve saved the API keys in a secure location and tap on the “***Done***” button.
6. **Start deploying and exploring data**. You can now start deploying Views and exploring the published data. For more details, check out “[Deploying Views](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/deploying-views).”


# FAQ

This section answers the most frequently asked questions and all things about Analog Watch.

<details>

<summary>How can I list a new smart contract on the Watch if it's not listed?</summary>

Check out "[Listing Smart Contracts](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/listing-smart-contracts)" to learn more about listing smart contracts on the Analog Watch.&#x20;

</details>

<details>

<summary>What blockchains does the  Analog Watch currently support?</summary>

For now, Analog Watch supports only Ethereum, Astar, and Polygon blockchains. We’ll soon be adding support for more blockchains. For more details, check out "[Supported Networks](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/supported-networks)."&#x20;

</details>

<details>

<summary>How do I join as a partner to test the Testnet?</summary>

[Contact us](https://www.analog.one/) or join the [Launch Partners Program](https://www.analog.one/partners-program).

</details>

<details>

<summary>At what time will the Analog Watch start charging the query fees?</summary>

The Analog Watch will continue evolving towards a decentralized Analog Watch. For this release, Analog Labs is handling most of the Watch operations, including smart contract listing and infrastructure maintenance. Once we roll out the mainnet, we expect data consumers to pay the query fees. See “[Rewards & Fees](/documentation/developers/analog-watch/developers-guide/rewards-and-fees)” for more details.

</details>

<details>

<summary>What wallet(s) works best with Analog Watch?</summary>

For now, any Substrate-based wallet should allow you to authenticate and authorize transactions on the Analog Watch. However, we intend to add more wallets in the future, including EVM-based ones, such as MetaMask. See [Supported Wallets](/documentation/analog-network/getting-started/supported-wallets) to learn more about a list of wallets that support the platform.

</details>

<details>

<summary>How do I contact the Analog team for support or info/questions/inquiries?</summary>

You can contact us through the following channels:

E-mail: <support@analog.one>

Discord: <https://discord.com/invite/analog>

Telegram: <https://t.me/analogtimer>

</details>


# Analog GMP

Analog GMP is a messaging protocol powered by the [Timechain](/documentation/developers/protocol-overview) and designed to facilitate the creation of [cross-chain dApps](https://www.analog.one/blog/introduction-to-cross-chain-smart-contract-execution-calls). Developers can use it to send [arbitrary messages (data),](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/developers-guide/tutorials/send-a-message-from-chain-a-to-chain-b) [tokens](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/developers-guide/tutorials/teleport-tokens-from-chain-a-to-chain-b),  and initiate actions across different blockchains.

As a general message-passing protocol, you can use Analog GMP to build any arbitrary cross-chain dApp. Developers are leveraging Analog GMP to build notable solutions such as:

* [**Zenswap**](https://zenswap.io/): An innovative solution for native cross-chain swaps that leverages USDC routing.
* [**PixelPort**](https://pixelport.xyz/): A novel NFT solution that maximizes NFT potential through cross-chain minting, fractionalization, and transfer.

Follow the [**Quickstart Guide** ](#quickstart)below to begin developing with Analog GMP.

{% hint style="success" %}

> You can initiate calls from EVM contracts or execute complex logic on the Timechain in response to a GMP message by  building on the Timechain EVM. For more details, see [Timechain EVM](/documentation/developers/timechain-evm).&#x20;
> {% endhint %}

***

## Quickstart

**Ready to build with Analog GMP?** This quickstart will get you up and running in minutes.

<table data-card-size="large" data-view="cards"><thead><tr><th></th><th></th><th data-hidden data-card-target data-type="content-ref"></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/developers-guide/getting-started"><strong>Getting Started</strong></a></td><td>Quickly deploy your first cross-chain message.</td><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/developers-guide/getting-started">Getting Started</a></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/build-your-first-omni-chain-dapp"><strong>Bridge Tokens With OATS</strong></a></td><td>Transfer tokens across any blockchain with a single, unified interface. </td><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/build-your-first-omni-chain-dapp">Build Your First Omni-Chain dApp</a></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components"><strong>Learn more about Analog GMP</strong></a></td><td>Explore the core components &#x26; architecture.</td><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components">Architectural Components</a></td></tr><tr><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/faq"><strong>FAQ</strong></a></td><td>Get answers to most frequently asked questions about Analog GMP.</td><td><a href="/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/faq">FAQ</a></td></tr></tbody></table>

***

## Core Features

Analog GMP is architected with the following features in mind:

<details>

<summary><strong>Permissionless interoperability</strong></summary>

Analog GMP is designed to be permissionless. Anyone can run the Timechain nodes that verify and execute cross-chain messages, and anyone can build dApps that integrate with it.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Defense-in-depth security</strong></summary>

The protocol's message transport layer is powered by the [Timechain](/documentation/developers/protocol-overview)—a sovereign blockchain that uses staked tokens for economic security. Threshold Signature Schemes (TSS) also provide another layer of security to Analog GMP.&#x20;

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Chain-Agnostic Design</strong></summary>

Analog GMP is designed to be chain-agnostic, empowering developers to build efficient cross-chain dApps across a wider range of blockchain networks.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Future-proof</strong></summary>

Analog GMP supports continuous updates, including onboarding new chains, adding new security features, and providing advanced functionalities like cross-chain automation.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Composability</strong></summary>

Analog GMP’s design allows for composable dApps, enabling developers to build as if on one chain. For example, [Zenswap’s](https://zenswap.io/) integration with Circle CCTP expands the design space for cross-chain DeFi with native USDC transfers.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Optimized gas payment mechanism</strong></summary>

Analog GMP uses several mechanisms to enhance gas efficiency for developers and users interacting with the gateways (e.g., an upgradeable proxy that is [EIP-1167](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-1167) and [EIP-1967-compliant](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-1967), [branchless optimizations](https://github.com/Analog-Labs/analog-gmp/blob/main/src/utils/BranchlessMath.sol), minimal on-chain storage, etc.). Check out this [post](https://www.analog.one/blog/gas-optimization-strategies-in-cross-chain-environments) for more details. &#x20;

</details>

***

# Architectural Components

The architectural components of Analog GMP can be grouped into two components: on-chain and off-chain.

## On-Chain Components

### Gateway Smart Contracts (GSCs)

A GSC is the primary contract that [shards](#shards) observe and which fundamentally facilitates cross-chain message passing. Analog Network supports one GSC on each blockchain, and this is the contract that the source and destination shard need to observe.  There are two categories of GSC interfaces:

1. **User-space interfaces**: These are interfaces that you’ll interact with. They can be grouped into two:
   1. [**`IGmpReceiver`**](#igmpreceiver-interface), which receives GMP messages on the destination chain’s ASC.
   2. [**`IGateway`**](#igateway-interface), which you’ll use to deposit funds, send GMP messages, and track GMP calls. &#x20;
2. **System-space interface**: This is the interface that the GMP protocol uses internally. It is implemented as [**`IExecutor`**](#iexecutor-interface) and handles the execution of GMP messages. Only the shards can call this interface.

#### IGmpReceiver Interface

The **`IGmpReceiver`** interface contains a single function [**`onGmpReceived`**](#function-ongmpreceived) that must be implemented by any contract wanting to receive GMP messages. Implement it to handle incoming messages and return a result.

<details>

<summary><strong>onGmpReceived</strong></summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function onGmpReceived(
    bytes32 id,
    uint128 network,
    bytes32 source,
    uint64 nonce,
    bytes calldata payload
) external payable returns (bytes32);
```

{% endcode %}

**Parameters:**

* `id` (bytes32): Unique identifier for the message (EIP-712 hash of the payload).
* `network` (uint128): Source chain ID (e.g., Ethereum Mainnet = 1).
* `source` (bytes32): Address/public key of the sender on the source chain.
* `nonce` (uint64): Unique number to prevent message replay.
* `payload` (bytes): Raw message data (decode this for your use case).

**Returns:**

* `bytes32`: 32-byte result (e.g., hash or status) stored with the GMP message.

{% hint style="warning" %}
Note that contracts implementing this interface must validate that the caller (`msg.sender`) is indeed the authorized Gateway Contract address to avoid unauthorized message handling or malicious interactions.&#x20;

Example:&#x20;

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
require(msg.sender == GATEWAY_ADDRESS, "Unauthorized");
```

{% endcode %}
{% endhint %}

</details>

#### IGateway Interface

The **`IGateway`** interface allows for cross-chain messaging, providing functions for message sending, gas cost calculation, and network details. It has three key functions (i.e., `submitMessage`, `estimateMessageCost`, and `networkId`) and one event (i.e., `GmpCreated`).

**Functions:**

<details>

<summary><strong><code>submitMessage</code></strong></summary>

Sends a cross-chain message.

* **Parameters**:
  * `destinationAddress` *(address)*: The target address on the destination blockchain.
  * `destinationNetwork` *(uint16)*: The identifier of the destination blockchain network.
  * `executionGasLimit` *(uint256)*: The maximum gas allowed for executing the message on the destination chain.
  * `data` *(bytes)*: Arbitrary message payload.
* **Returns**:  A unique message identifier (`bytes32`) used to track the submitted message.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong><code>estimateMessageCost</code></strong></summary>

Estimates how much gas (cost) is required to execute a cross-chain message.

**Parameters:**

* `networkid` *(uint16)*: The target blockchain network ID.
* `messageSize` *(uint256)*: Size of the message payload in bytes.
* `gasLimit` *(uint256)*: Desired gas limit for executing the message.

**Returns:**

* Estimated cost (`uint256`) of executing the message.

**`estimateMessageCost`** returns a value that serves as a `msg.value` parameter of the gateway’s [submitMessage](#submitmessage) function. You can use this value (i.e., `msg.value`) to pass in the native token of your origin chain as the gas fee. For example, you can call the [`submitMessage`](#submitmessage) function like this:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
gateway.submitMessage{value: msg.value}(dest_address, dest_network, GMP_GAS_LIMIT, payload);

```

{% endcode %}

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong><code>networkId</code></strong></summary>

Returns the identifier (`uint16`) of the current blockchain network.

**Usage Example:**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
uint16 currentNetwork = gateway.networkId();

```

{% endcode %}

</details>

**Events:**

<details>

<summary><strong><code>GmpCreated</code></strong></summary>

Emitted whenever a new cross-chain message is successfully submitted.

**Event Parameters:**

* `id` *(bytes32 indexed)*: Unique identifier for the message (EIP-712 hash).
* `source` *(bytes32 indexed)*: Sender's account identifier (with a flag indicating contract or EOA).
* `destinationAddress` *(address indexed)*: Target address on the destination chain.
* `destinationNetwork` *(uint16)*: Target blockchain network ID.
* `executionGasLimit` *(uint64)*: Gas limit set for execution on the target chain.
* `gasCost` *(uint64)*: Gas cost associated with executing this message.
* `nonce` *(uint64)*: Unique sequence number per sender to ensure uniqueness of messages.
* `data` *(bytes)*: Arbitrary data payload sent in the message.

{% hint style="warning" %}
**Note:**&#x20;

* Ensure sufficient funds (`msg.value`) are provided when calling `submitMessage` to cover gas costs.
* Always use `estimateMessageCost` before submitting messages to avoid transaction failures due to insufficient gas.
  {% endhint %}

</details>

#### IExecutor Interface

The **`IExecutor`** interface defines functions and events for executing GMP messages, managing shard keys, and configuring routes within the GSC.&#x20;

{% hint style="warning" %}
The GMP protocol relies on this interface for internal operations, and only Timechain shards can call it.
{% endhint %}

**Functions:**

There are three categories of functions: **Message Execution**, **Shard Management, and Route Configuration.**&#x20;

1. **Message Execution**

<details>

<summary><strong><code>execute</code></strong></summary>

Handles the execution of a single GMP message.

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function execute(Signature calldata signature, GmpMessage calldata message)
    external returns (GmpStatus status, bytes32 result)
```

{% endcode %}

* Verifies TSS signature
* Executes target contract call
* Returns execution status (Success/Revert)

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong><code>batchExecute</code></strong></summary>

Execute operations/messages in batch with signature verification:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function batchExecute(Signature calldata signature, InboundMessage calldata message) external

```

{% endcode %}

* Executes a batch of commands (GMP messages, shard updates)
* Requires valid TSS signature for the entire batch

</details>

2. **Shard Management**

<details>

<summary><strong><code>Shard Management</code></strong></summary>

**List currently registered shards:**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function shards() external returns (TssKey[] memory);
```

{% endcode %}

Returns an array of currently registered shard TSS keys.

**Register a single shard key:**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function setShard(TssKey calldata publicKey) external;
```

{% endcode %}

Registers a single shard TSS key.

**Register multiple shard keys at once:**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function setShards(TssKey[] calldata publicKeys) external;
```

{% endcode %}

Registers multiple shard TSS keys in batch.

**Revoke (unregister) a single shard key:**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function revokeShard(TssKey calldata publicKey) external;
```

{% endcode %}

Revokes/unregisters a specific shard TSS key.

**Revoke multiple shard keys at once:**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function revokeShards(TssKey[] calldata publicKey) external;
```

{% endcode %}

Revokes multiple shard TSS keys simultaneously.

</details>

3. **Route Configuration**

<details>

<summary><strong><code>Route Configuration</code></strong></summary>

**List currently registered routes:**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function routes() external returns (Route[] memory);
```

{% endcode %}

Returns an array of currently registered routes within the gateway.

**Register or update a single route:**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function setRoute(Route calldata info) external;
```

{% endcode %}

Creates or updates a single route entry.

**Register or update multiple routes at once:**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function setRoutes(Route[] calldata values) external;
```

{% endcode %}

Creates or updates multiple route entries simultaneously.

</details>

**Events:**

The interface defines several events to emit important state changes:

<details>

<summary><strong><code>GmpExecuted</code></strong></summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
event GmpExecuted(
    bytes32 indexed id,
    GmpSender indexed source,
    address indexed dest,
    GmpStatus status,
    bytes32 result
);
```

{% endcode %}

Emitted whenever a GMP message is executed.

* **`id`**: EIP-712 hash uniquely identifying the GMP payload.
* **`source`**: Sender's public key or address (format chain-dependent).
* **`dest`**: Recipient's Ethereum address.
* **`status`**: Execution status of the GMP message.
* **`result`**: Resulting data or hash from execution.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong><code>BatchExecuted</code></strong></summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
event BatchExecuted(uint64 batch);
```

{% endcode %}

Emitted when a batch of operations/messages is executed.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong><code>ShardsRegistered</code></strong></summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
event ShardsRegistered(TssKey[] keys);
```

{% endcode %}

Emitted when new shard keys are registered.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong><code>ShardsUnregistered</code></strong></summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
event ShardsUnregistered(TssKey[] keys);
```

{% endcode %}

Emitted when existing shard keys are revoked/unregistered.

</details>

## Application Smart Contracts (ASCs)

A smart contract deployed by applications on the source and destination chains. An ASC can be your cross-chain dApp or an existing ecosystem protocol. To create a [cross-chain smart contract execution call](https://www.analog.one/blog/a-deep-dive-into-cross-chain-smart-contract-execution-calls), you'll need to:

* Use the [**`IGateway`**](#igateway-interface) interface in the source chain's ASC to call the [**`submitMessage`**](#submitmessage) function on the source Gateway contract
* Implement the [**`IGmpReceiver`**](#igmpreceiver-interface) interface on the destination chain's ASC to receive the cross-chain message in the [**`onGmpReceived`**](#ongmpreceived) function.

### Time Nodes

Validators that leverage the NPoS consensus protocol to secure the Timechain. See [Timechain](/documentation/analog-network/overview#timechain) for more details.

## Off-Chain Components

### Chronicle Nodes

Validators that observe external chains for certain state changes, transactions, or events associated with a registered smart contract address and report them to the Timechain. For more details, read the [Timepaper](https://www.analog.one/Analog-Timepaper.pdf).&#x20;

### Shards

A process that Analog uses to parallelize the Chronicle Nodes network into partitions—called shards—to achieve multi-chain operability. Read the [Timepaper](https://www.analog.one/Analog-Timepaper.pdf) for more details.&#x20;

{% hint style="success" %}
Analog GMP relies on the Timechain to track and manage cross-chain requests by coordinating t**wo shard committees.** One source chain committee confirms finality and reports to the Timechain, and the destination chain committee executes the request. Both committees use Gateway Smart Contracts, with their public keys registered on the Timechain.
{% endhint %}

### Threshold Signature Scheme (TSS)

A type of digital signature protocol that allows chronicle nodes to establish subsets such that only a certain subset of the group can generate signatures on behalf of the group when handling cross-chain requests via the GMP protocol.  Read the [Timepaper](https://www.analog.one/Analog-Timepaper.pdf) for more details.&#x20;


# Transaction Lifecycle

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FolRxviiX0LBwMdviLDCO%2FGMP%20Overview.png?alt=media&amp;token=1d93614f-1da9-4a96-bf39-8dc5b8b235c9" alt=""><figcaption><p>Transaction Lifecycle</p></figcaption></figure>

The transaction flow for cross-chain message transmission involves three key components: **Gateway**, **Executor**, and **GMP Receiver.**

## Source Chain

1. **Message Initiation:** A user or contract on the source chain calls the [submitMessage() ](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#submitmessage)function on the [`IGateway`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#igateway-interface) interface, providing:
   1. Destination address
   2. Destination network ID
   3. Execution gas limit
   4. Message payload data
2. **Message Processing:** The Gateway:
   1. Calculates the gas cost using [estimateMessageCost()](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#estimatemessagecost)
   2. Generates a unique message ID (EIP-712 hash)
   3. Assigns a sequential `nonce` to ensure uniqueness
   4. Emits a [GmpCreated](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#gmpcreated) event with all message details
   5. Returns the message ID to the caller

## Cross-Chain Transmission

3. **Message Relay**: Behind the scenes, the message is picked up by the Timechain:
   1. The message details are observed by Timechain shards (Chronicle Nodes)
   2. Shards validate and sign the message using signatures
   3. The Timechain prepares the message for delivery to the destination chain

## Destination Chain

4. **Message Execution:** On the destination chain, the [`IExecutor`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#iexecutor-interface) interface:

   1. Receives the signed message via [**`execute`**](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#execute) or [`batchExecute()`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#batch-execution) with a signature and `InboundMessage`
   2. Verifies the signature against registered shards (TSS keys)
   3. Processes the message according to registered routes
   4. Forwards the message to the appropriate destination address

5. **Message Receipt:** The destination contract (implementing [IGmpReceiver](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#igmpreceiver-interface)):

   1. Receives the message via [onGmpReceived() ](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#function-ongmpreceived)function
   2. Validates that the caller is the authorized Gateway contract
   3. Processes the message payload
   4. Returns a 32-byte result

6. **Execution Confirmation**: The Executor:
   1. Captures the execution result
   2. Updates the message status
   3. Emits a [GmpExecuted](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#gmpexecuted) event with the message ID, source, destination, status, and result
   4. Emits a [BatchExecuted](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#batchexecuted) event for the batch containing the message


# Time to Finality

Different chains employ varying consensus protocols, resulting in different finality times. To ensure transaction security, Analog GMP’s attestation service requires a certain number of block confirmations/time to finality on the connected source chain before transactions can be considered finalized.

This number varies based on the connected chain, as summarized in the table below:

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="Mainnet Beta" %}
Coming soon
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="Testnet" %}

| Source chain     | Block confirmation count | Estimated time to finality |
| ---------------- | ------------------------ | -------------------------- |
| Ethereum Sepolia | \~5                      | \~60 seconds               |
| Astar Shibuya    | 1                        | \~35 seconds               |
| Binance Testnet  | \~3                      | \~ 5 seconds               |
| Polygon PoS Amoy | 1                        | \~20 seconds               |
| Arbitrum Sepolia | \~5                      | \~20 seconds               |
| {% endtab %}     |                          |                            |
| {% endtabs %}    |                          |                            |

{% hint style="info" %}
Note that the figures in the table above are averages across multiple transactions. These estimates can vary significantly due to many factors, including network conditions and the specific blockchain. For example, for L2s like Arbitrum, the time to finality depends on the time it takes to publish transaction data to their underlying L1 blockchain (Ethereum in this case). In this instance, Analog GMP must wait for the L1 blockchain to finalize.
{% endhint %}


# Fee Management

Analog GMP offers developers a simple, one-time fee model. Pay once in the source chain's native token to cover all costs for message delivery and execution on any destination chain.

## **Fee Components**

The total fee you pay via `msg.value` when you submit a message is an aggregate of three distinct costs:

* **Destination Execution Cost:** Gas required to run your [`onGmpReceived`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#ongmpreceived) function on the destination chain.
* **Protocol & Network Fee:** A fee paid to the Analog network for cross-chain verification and security.
* **Relayer (Chronicle) Compensation:** Incentive for the off-chain actor submitting the transaction.

## The Fee Lifecycle

The process involves estimating the cost, paying it on the source chain, and the subsequent use of those funds to compensate the relayer on the destination chain.

### Step 1: Estimate the Fee on the Source Chain

To send a message, first calculate the required fee by calling the `estimateMessageCost` function on the source chain's Gateway contract.

<details>

<summary><strong><code>estimateMessageCost</code></strong></summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
function estimateMessageCost(
    uint16 network,
    uint16 messageSize,
    uint64 gasLimit
) external view returns (uint256);
```

{% endcode %}

The calculation is a two-part process:

**A. Calculating an Abstract Gas Cost**

First, the protocol computes a normalized gas amount that accounts for both the message payload overhead and the execution gas limit you specify.

The formula is:&#x20;

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
TotalGas=(messageSize×gasCoef1)+gasCoef0+gasLimit
```

{% endcode %}

Where:

* `gasLimit`: The gas you want to reserve for your [`onGmpReceived`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#ongmpreceived) function's execution.
* `messageSize`: The size of your message payload in bytes.
* `gasCoef0` & `gasCoef1`: Route-specific constants that represent the fixed and variable overhead gas costs for that particular cross-chain route.

**B. Converting Gas to a Token Fee**

Next, this abstract `TotalGas` is converted into a fee in the source chain's native token.

The formula is:&#x20;

$$Fee=  relativeGasPriceDenominator (TotalGas\*relativeGasPriceNumerator) ​  +baseFee$$&#x20;

Where:

* $$relativeGasPriceNumerator / relativeGasPriceDenominator$$: This ratio acts as an exchange rate between the destination and source chain gas prices, handling price volatility and differences.
* $$baseFee$$: A flat protocol fee for the service, denominated in the source chain's native token.

The resulting $$Fee$$ is the amount you must attach to your [`submitMessage`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#submitmessage) call.

</details>

### Step 2: Paying the Fee on the Source Chain

When you call [`submitMessage`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#submitmessage), you provide the calculated fee as the `msg.value`. The `Gateway` contract validates if the payment is sufficient:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
uint256 fee = route.estimateCost(gas);
require(msg.value >= fee, "insufficient tx value");
```

{% endcode %}

If the payment is sufficient, your message is accepted, a [`GmpCreated`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#gmpcreated) event is emitted, and the fee is held by the Gateway contract on the source chain.

### Step 3: Relayer Compensation and Gas Refund on the Destination Chain

1. **Chronicle (relayer):**
   * Detects [`GmpCreated`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#gmpcreated)
   * Pays gas to call [`execute()`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#execute) on destination Gateway
2. **Refund Mechanism in** [**`execute()`**](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#execute)**:**

   ```solidity
   uint256 initialGas = gasleft();  // Start gas tracking
   // ... executes onGmpReceived ...
   uint256 gasUsed = initialGas - gasleft(); 
   uint256 refund = (gasUsed * tx.gasprice).min(address(this).balance);

   // Send refund to relayer
   (bool success,) = caller().call{value: refund}("");
   ```

   * The [`execute`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#execute) function calculates how much gas was consumed from start to finish.
   * It multiplies the `gasUsed` by the transaction's gas price (`tx.gasprice`) to get the total cost the Chronicle paid for the transaction in the destination chain's native currency.
   * It then  transfers this `refund` amount from the `Gateway` contract's balance to the Chronicle (`caller()`).
