# Foundry

In this tutorial, you'll create, deploy, and interact with a cross-chain token using the Analog OATS standard. You will write and deploy a smart contract that burns tokens on Arbitrum Sepolia and, using the OATS protocol, mint an equivalent amount on Ethereum Sepolia.

## **Learning Objectives**

* Set up a Foundry project for development on Arbitrum and Ethereum Sepolia.
* Install and manage Analog GMP & OATS as project dependencies.
* Implement a burn-and-mint model for cross-chain token transfers.
* Deploy and test your token on live testnets.

***

## **Prerequisites**

Before you begin, you will need:

* **Foundry:** Follow these [steps](https://getfoundry.sh/introduction/installation/) to install it.
* **Node.js:** Required for managing project dependencies.
* **Wallet & Testnet Funds:** A wallet like MetaMask funded with both Arbitrum Sepolia ETH and Ethereum Sepolia ETH from [public faucets](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/developers-guide/getting-started#prerequisites).
* **RPC URLs:** Endpoints for Arbitrum Sepolia and Ethereum Sepolia from a node provider like Alchemy or Infura.&#x20;

***

## **Step 1: Initialize Your Foundry Project**

Create a new directory for your project and initialize it with Foundry.

```bash
mkdir analog-oats-tutorial
cd analog-oats-tutorial
forge init
```

This creates the standard Foundry project structure (`src/`, `lib/`, `test/`).

***

## **Step 2: Install Dependencies**

Next, install the required OATS and OpenZeppelin contracts using `forge`.

```bash
# Install OpenZeppelin contracts
forge install OpenZeppelin/openzeppelin-contracts

# Install Analog OATS and GMP contracts
forge install Analog-Labs/oats 
forge install Analog-Labs/analog-gmp 
```

{% hint style="success" %}
If the `forge install` commands fail, you can use `git submodules` as a fallback: `git submodule add https://github.com/Analog-Labs/oats lib/oats` `git submodule add https://github.com/Analog-Labs/analog-gmp lib/analog-gmp`
{% endhint %}

After installing the libraries, open `foundry.toml` and add the following remappings so the compiler can locate the dependencies:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
[profile.default]
src = "src"
out = "out"
libs = ["lib"]
remappings = [
    '@openzeppelin/contracts/=lib/openzeppelin-contracts/contracts/',
    'oats/=lib/oats/src/',
    'gmp/=lib/analog-gmp/src/'
]
```

{% endcode %}

***

## **Step 3: Create the Smart Contract**

Create a new file at `src/CrossChainOats.sol`. This contract will:

* Inherit from OpenZeppelin's `ERC20Burnable` and `ERC20Capped`.
* Implement the `ISender` and `IGmpReceiver` interfaces.
* Burn tokens to initiate a cross-chain transfer.
* Receive messages to mint tokens on the destination chain.

Copy the following code into `src/CrossChainOats.sol`:

<details>

<summary><strong><code>CrossChainOats.sol</code></strong></summary>

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.20;

import {ISender, Utils} from "oats/IOATS.sol";
import {IGateway} from "gmp/IGateway.sol";
import {IGmpReceiver} from "gmp/IGmpReceiver.sol";

import {Ownable} from "@openzeppelin/contracts/access/Ownable.sol";
import {ERC20} from "@openzeppelin/contracts/token/ERC20/ERC20.sol";
import {ERC20Burnable} from "@openzeppelin/contracts/token/ERC20/extensions/ERC20Burnable.sol";
import {ERC20Capped} from "@openzeppelin/contracts/token/ERC20/extensions/ERC20Capped.sol";

/// @title CrossChainOats
/// @notice An OATS-compliant token for cross-chain transfers using a burn/mint model.
contract CrossChainOats is ISender, IGmpReceiver, Ownable, ERC20Burnable, ERC20Capped {
    
    IGateway private immutable _gateway;
    mapping(uint16 => address) public networks;
    uint64 private constant GAS_LIMIT = 100_000;

    struct TransferCmd {
        address from;
        address to;
        uint256 amount;
    }

    constructor(string memory name, string memory symbol, uint256 cap, address gateway)
        ERC20(name, symbol)
        Ownable(msg.sender)
        ERC20Capped(cap)
    {
        _gateway = IGateway(gateway);
    }

    /// @notice Links this contract to its counterpart on another network.
    function setNetwork(uint16 networkId, address token) public onlyOwner {
        networks[networkId] = token;
    }

    /// @inheritdoc ISender
    function cost(uint16 networkId) external view returns (uint256) {
        // Corresponds to the encoded TransferCmd struct (32 * 3 = 96 bytes).
        return _gateway.estimateMessageCost(networkId, 96, GAS_LIMIT);
    }

    /// @inheritdoc ISender
    /// @notice Burns tokens and sends a message to mint on the destination chain.
    function send(uint16 networkId, address recipient, uint256 amount) external payable returns (bytes32 msgId) {
        address targetToken = networks[networkId];
        if (targetToken == address(0)) {
            revert Utils.UnknownNetwork(networkId);
        }

        _burn(msg.sender, amount);

        bytes memory message = abi.encode(TransferCmd({from: msg.sender, to: recipient, amount: amount}));
        
        // `msg.value` covers the GMP fee.
        return _gateway.submitMessage{value: msg.value}(targetToken, networkId, GAS_LIMIT, message);
    }

    /// @inheritdoc IGmpReceiver
    /// @notice Called by the Gateway to mint tokens when a cross-chain message is received.
    function onGmpReceived(bytes32 id, uint128 networkId, bytes32 source, uint64, bytes calldata data)
        external
        payable
        returns (bytes32)
    {
        require(msg.sender == address(_gateway), "Unauthorized: caller is not the Gateway");
        
        address sourceToken = address(uint160(uint256(source)));
        require(networks[uint16(networkId)] == sourceToken, "Unauthorized: unknown source token");

        TransferCmd memory cmd = abi.decode(data, (TransferCmd));
        _mint(cmd.to, cmd.amount);

        return id;
    }

    /// @inheritdoc ERC20Capped
    function _update(address from, address to, uint256 value) internal override(ERC20, ERC20Capped) {
        super._update(from, to, value);
    }
}
```

{% endcode %}

</details>

***

## **Step 4: Compile the Contract**

Run the `build` command to compile your contract and generate ABI artifacts.

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
forge build
```

{% endcode %}

***

## **Step 5: Set Up Environment Variables**

Create a `.env` file in your root directory to securely store your private key and RPC URLs. **Never commit this file to version control.**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
# .env
ETH_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL="<YOUR_RPC_URL>"
ARBITRUM_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL="<YOUR_RPC_URL>"
PRIVATE_KEY="<YOUR_WALLET_PRIVATE_KEY>"
```

{% endcode %}

Next, add `.env` to your `.gitignore` file.

```bash
echo ".env" >> .gitignore
```

***

## **Step 6: Deploy to Networks**

Before deploying, you must get the official **Gateway addresses & Network IDs**. See [**Gateway Addresses & Network IDs**](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/gateway-addresses-and-network-ids) for more details.&#x20;

1. Load your environment variables into your current shell session.

```bash
source .env
```

2. **Deploy to Arbitrum Sepolia**

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
forge create --rpc-url $ARBITRUM_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL --private-key $PRIVATE_KEY \
  src/CrossChainOats.sol:CrossChainOats \
  --constructor-args "My OmniChain Token" "MOT" 1000000000000000000000000 "<ARB_GATEWAY_ADDRESS>" \
  --verify 
```

{% endcode %}

**Save the deployed contract address.** Let's call it `ARB_CONTRACT_ADDRESS`.

3. **Deploy to Ethereum Sepolia.**&#x20;

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
forge create --rpc-url $ETH_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL --private-key $PRIVATE_KEY \
  src/CrossChainOats.sol:CrossChainOats \
  --constructor-args "My OmniChain Token" "MOT" 1000000000000000000000000 "<ETH_GATEWAY_ADDRESS>" \
  --verify 
```

{% endcode %}

**Save the deployed contract address.** Let's call it `ETH_CONTRACT_ADDRESS`.

***

## **Step 7: Configure and Test the Cross-Chain Transfer**

Now you will link the two contracts and perform a transfer from Arbitrum to Ethereum.

**1. Link the Contracts.** You need to tell each contract about its counterpart on the other network.

* **On Arbitrum, link to the Ethereum contract:**

  ```bash
  cast send $ARB_CONTRACT_ADDRESS "setNetwork(uint16,address)" 11155111 $ETH_CONTRACT_ADDRESS \
    --rpc-url $ARBITRUM_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL --private-key $PRIVATE_KEY
  ```
* **On Ethereum, link to the Arbitrum contract:**

  ```bash
  cast send $ETH_CONTRACT_ADDRESS "setNetwork(uint16,address)" 421614 $ARB_CONTRACT_ADDRESS \
    --rpc-url $ETH_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL --private-key $PRIVATE_KEY
  ```

**2. Initiate the Transfer (Arbitrum → Ethereum)** First, estimate the fee required for the GMP message by calling the `cost` function on the Arbitrum contract.

```bash
cast call $ARB_CONTRACT_ADDRESS "cost(uint16)" 11155111 --rpc-url $ARBITRUM_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL
```

This command will output the required fee in wei. **Copy this value for the next step.**

Now, execute the `send` function to burn 100 tokens. You must pass the fee you just retrieved as the transaction's `value`. Replace `<RECIPIENT_ADDRESS>` with the destination wallet and `<GMP_FEE>` with the value you copied.

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
# We are sending 100 CCT (100 * 10^18)
cast send $ARB_CONTRACT_ADDRESS "send(uint16,address,uint256)" 11155111 <RECIPIENT_ADDRESS> 100000000000000000000 \
  --value <GMP_FEE> \
  --rpc-url $ARBITRUM_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL --private-key $PRIVATE_KEY
```

{% endcode %}

**3. Verify the Results**

* **Check Balance on Arbitrum:** Your token balance on Arbitrum Sepolia should have decreased by 100.

  Bash

  ```bash
  cast call $ARB_CONTRACT_ADDRESS "balanceOf(address)" <YOUR_WALLET_ADDRESS> --rpc-url $ARBITRUM_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL
  ```
* **Wait for OATS Processing:** The Analog network will process the message, which may take a few minutes.
* **Check Balance on Ethereum:** Once the message is delivered, the [`onGmpReceived`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#ongmpreceived) function mints 100 tokens to the recipient on Ethereum Sepolia.

  ```bash
  cast call $ETH_CONTRACT_ADDRESS "balanceOf(address)" <RECIPIENT_ADDRESS> --rpc-url $ETH_SEPOLIA_RPC_URL
  ```

***

Congratulations! You have successfully built and tested a dApp that performs a cross-chain token transfer using OATS.

# Remix

This [guide](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/build-your-first-omni-chain-dapp) outlines the process of deploying an OATS-compliant smart contract using the Remix IDE. You will learn to:

* **Set up your project:** Initialize a smart contract project within the Remix IDE, configured for a supported network.
* **Integrate Analog GMP & OATS:** Utilize the Analog GMP & OATS protocols within your smart contract to implement a burn-and-mint mechanism for cross-chain token transfers.
* **Deploy and Test:** Deploy your smart contract to a supported network and conduct tests to ensure its functionality.


# Protocol Architecture

##


# Overview of OATS

OATS is an omnichain token standard that enables you to send and receive tokens and other data between smart contracts on different blockchains.

### Why Use OATS?

Traditionally, extending a token's utility beyond its home blockchain was a complex and manual task for developers. OATS is designed to solve these challenges.

**Before OATS:**

* **Complex Infrastructure:** Required deploying and managing separate token contracts or liquidity pools on every new chain.
* **Difficult Integrations:** Involved complex work with various bridges, each having unique security protocols.
* **Slow Development:** Manually extending token features to new chains was a time-consuming process.

**With OATS, you can:**

* **Deploy with Ease:** Quickly make new or existing tokens ready for cross-chain operations.
* **Enhance Security:** Use the decentralized and secure Analog General Message Passing (GMP) for all token transfers.
* **Gain Flexibility:** Select the best transfer model for your token, such as a `burn/mint` or `lock/unlock` mechanism.
* **Unlock New Possibilities:** Trigger smart contract interactions on a destination chain immediately following a token transfer, enabling advanced DeFi and dApp features.

***

### Key Concepts

OATS is built on top of Analog's core components: the [**Timechain**](/documentation/developers/protocol-overview#the-timechain-analogs-core) and [Analog GMP](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp).&#x20;

* **Timechain:** Serves as a decentralized and immutable ledger, providing the ultimate source of security and verification for all cross-chain activities.
* **Analog GMP:** Serves as the transport system for cross-chain data.
* **OATS :** This is the application layer.&#x20;

{% hint style="success" %}
While OATS uses Analog GMP to send messages, it provides the specific rules, interfaces (`IOATS.sol`), and base implementations (`TokenSender.sol`)  and **`TokenSenderCallers.sol`** needed to create a unified omni-chain application. In essence, OATS defines *what* the application does, while Analog GMP handles *how* the cross-chain message is delivered.
{% endhint %}

***

## OATS Models

OATS provides two distinct models for managing cross-chain token transfers: **Burn/Mint** and **Lock/Unlock**. The selection of a model is based on the token's architecture and the desired level of control over its supply mechanics.

* **Burn/Mint:**
  * **Suitable for:** Newly deployed tokens or tokens where the developer controls the total supply mechanics across chains.
  * **Mechanism:** Tokens are burned on the source chain, and an equivalent amount is minted on the destination chain. This requires the token contract to have minting and burning capabilities (e.g., `ERC20Burnable` and controlled minting, often `ERC20Capped` is used to manage total supply).
  * **Implementation:** Your token contract directly inherits [`ISender`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/protocol-architecture/how-oats-works#isender-interface)/[`ISenderCaller`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/protocol-architecture/how-oats-works#isendercaller-interface) and [`IGmpReceiver`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#igmpreceiver-interface).
* **Lock/Unlock:**
  * **Suitable for:** Existing tokens, especially those with a fixed supply that cannot be minted or burned arbitrarily.
  * **Mechanism:** Tokens sent from the source chain are locked in a dedicated **OATS Bridge contract**. When these tokens arrive on the destination chain, an equivalent amount of tokens (which were previously locked from another transfer or pre-supplied) are unlocked from the **OATS Bridge contract** on the destination chain and sent to the recipient.
  * **Implementation:** Requires a separate **Bridge contract** that implements [`ISender`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/protocol-architecture/how-oats-works#isender-interface)/[`ISenderCaller`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/protocol-architecture/how-oats-works#isendercaller-interface) and [`IGmpReceiver`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#igmpreceiver-interface). This Bridge contract holds the locked tokens.

***


# How OATS Works

OATS facilitates cross-chain token transfers by defining a set of interfaces that your token (or an associated bridge contract) implements. These interfaces interact with the Analog GMP Gateway contract.

***

## Key Components & Process

A cross-chain transfer occurs in three main stages: initiation on the source chain, relay by the Timechain, and execution on the destination chain.

### **1. Setup: Implement OATS Interfaces**

To make your token compatible with OATS, your contracts must implement specific interfaces:

1. **Source Chain Contract**: This is typically your main token contract or a dedicated bridge contract. It must implement [`ISender`](#isender-interface) for simple transfers or [`ISenderCaller`](#isendercaller-interface) for transfers that trigger a subsequent function call.

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="ISender Interface" %}
{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```solidity
interface ISender {
    /// @notice Get GMP message cost for cross-chain tokens transfer
    /// @param networkId Chain Id of the target network
    function cost(uint16 networkId) external view returns (uint256);
    /// @notice Transfer tokens to another network.
    /// Note this is payable, caller should transfer GMP fee amount provided by cost function above
    /// @param networkId Chain Id of the target network
    /// @param recipient address of the recipient account
    /// @param amount amount to transfer
    /// @return msgId GMP message Id
    function send(uint16 networkId, address recipient, uint256 amount) external payable returns (bytes32 msgId);
}
```

{% endcode %}
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="ISenderCaller Interface" %}

```solidity
interface ISenderCaller {
    function cost(uint16 networkId, uint256 gasLimit, bytes memory caldata) external view returns (uint256);

    function sendAndCall(
        uint16 networkId,
        address recipient,
        uint256 amount,
        uint256 gasLimit,
        address callee,
        bytes memory caldata
    ) external payable returns (bytes32 msgId);
}
```

{% endtab %}
{% endtabs %}

2. **Destination Chain Contract**: This contract, which will receive the transfer, must implement the `IGmpReceiver` interface. See [**IGmpReceiver Interface** ](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#igmpreceiver-interface)for more details.&#x20;

### **2. The Transfer Flow**

**Step 1: Initiation (Source Chain):** A user initiates a transfer by calling either `send()` or `sendAndCall()` on the source chain contract.

1. **User Action**: The user specifies the destination chain, recipient address, and token amount. They also pay a fee to cover the cross-chain transaction cost.
2. **Source Contract's Logic**:
   * Handles the token state change on the source chain (e.g., burning the tokens or locking them in the contract).
   * Constructs a message payload with all the transfer details.
   * Finally, it calls [`submitMessage()`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#submitmessage) on the Analog GMP Gateway, passing the payload and fee.

**Step 2: Relay (Analog GMP)** The Analog network securely relays the transfer message.

1. **Event Emission**: The source chain's GMP Gateway emits a [`GmpCreated` ](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#gmpcreated)event.
2. **Validation**: The Timechain observes this event, and its validators reach a consensus to approve the message, generating a TSS signature.
3. **Relay**: A Timechain node submits the signed message to the GMP Gateway on the destination chain by calling its [`execute()`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#execute) or [`batchExecute()`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#batchexecute) function.

**Step 3: Execution (Destination Chain)** The GMP Gateway on the destination chain completes the process.

1. **Verification**: The Gateway verifies the TSS signature from the Timechain.
2. **Receiving the Transfer**: Upon successful verification, the Gateway calls the [`onGmpReceived()`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#ongmpreceived) function on your destination contract.
3. **Destination's Contract's Logic**:
   * It decodes the message payload to get the transfer details.
   * It performs security checks, such as verifying the source chain.
   * It completes the transfer by minting new tokens for the recipient or unlocking tokens from the contract.
   * If the original call was `sendAndCall`, your contract can now execute the specified function call on a target contract.

***


# Requirements for OATS Integration

To integrate your tokens with OATS, follow these steps.

## 1. Get the Gateway Addresses

First, you'll need the deployed address of the **Analog GMP Gateway** contract for each blockchain you plan to support. You can find these addresses in the [**Gateway Addresses & Network IDs**](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/gateway-addresses-and-network-ids) page.

***

## 2. Choose Your Token Model

Decide how your token will move between chains. You have two options:

* **Burn/Mint Model:** This model burns tokens on the source chain and mints an equivalent amount on the destination chain.
* **Lock/Unlock Model:** This model locks tokens in a contract on the source chain and unlocks a corresponding amount of pre-existing tokens on the destination chain.

***

## 3. Implement the Smart Contract

<details>

<summary><strong>For a Burn/Mint Model:</strong></summary>

Your token contract must:

1. **Inherit Interfaces:** Inherit [`ISender`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/protocol-architecture/how-oats-works#isender-interface) (or [`ISenderCaller`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/protocol-architecture/how-oats-works#isendercaller-interface)) and [`IGmpReceiver`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#igmpreceiver-interface).
2. **Implement Functions:** Add the `cost()`, `send()` (or `sendAndCall()`), and `onGmpReceived()` functions.
3. **Set Access Control:** Ensure only authorized accounts can mint or burn tokens. For example, use `Ownable` to manage network settings and restrict `onGmpReceived()` calls to the GMP Gateway.
4. **Check Token Caps:** If you're using `ERC20Capped`, make sure the cap is high enough to handle cross-chain minting.

See [**Build Your First Omni-Chain dApp**](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/build-your-first-omni-chain-dapp#burn-mint) for more details.&#x20;

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>For a Lock/Unlock Model:</strong></summary>

You'll need a separate **Bridge contract** that:

1. **Inherits Interfaces:** Implements [`ISender`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/protocol-architecture/how-oats-works#isender-interface) (or [`ISenderCaller`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/protocol-architecture/how-oats-works#isendercaller-interface)) and [`IGmpReceiver`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#igmpreceiver-interface).
2. **Manages Tokens:** Handles the locking and unlocking of the underlying ERC20 token. The original token contract doesn't need any changes.

See [**Build Your First Omni-Chain dApp** ](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats/build-your-first-omni-chain-dapp#lock-unlock)for more details.&#x20;

</details>

***

## 4. Configure Your Network

Your contract needs a way to map each `networkId` to the correct token or bridge contract address on other chains. You can manage this with a mapping that the contract owner can update.

***

## 5. Set Gas Limits

You must specify a `GAS_LIMIT` for the [`onGmpReceived`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#ongmpreceived) function on the destination chain. While a starting value of `100,000` is suggested in the examples, you should test and adjust this based on your contract's specific needs and complexity.


# Best Practices for Secure and Efficient OATS Integration

While the [Timechain](/documentation/developers/protocol-overview#the-timechain-analogs-core) provides a secure foundation with features like TSS and NPoS, you are responsible for securing your own dApp. To build secure and efficient smart contracts on Analog, consider the following:

* **Security First:**
  * **Ownable and Access Control:** Use `Ownable` or a similar access control pattern to protect administrative functions like `set_network`. Only the owner should be able to change critical addresses.
  * **Validate the Gateway:** The most critical check in your [`onGmpReceived`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#ongmpreceived) function is `require(msg.sender == address(_gateway), "Unauthorized")`. This ensures that mint/unlock operations can only be initiated by the trusted Analog GMP Gateway.
  * **Input Validation:** Sanitize all inputs to prevent unexpected behavior.
* **Gas Efficiency:**
  * The `cost()` function helps estimate the fee required for the cross-chain transaction. Ensure your frontend can fetch this value and inform the user.
  * Keep the logic inside [`onGmpReceived`](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components#ongmpreceived) as lean as possible to minimize gas consumption on the destination chain.
* **Modularity:**
  * Separate your core token logic from the OATS interoperability logic. The Lock/Unlock model is a good example, where a dedicated `Bridge` contract handles all OATS interactions, leaving the original `ERC20` token untouched.

Automate your dApp workflows with Analog Automation, a decentralized platform for event-driven smart contract execution across multiple blockchains. It allows you to build dApps that automatically respond to on-chain and off-chain events with actions like trades, payments, and data updates. Key features of Analog Automation include:

* **Event-driven execution:** React to real-time events (e.g., price thresholds, NFT sales) with automated scripts.
* **Cross-chain interoperability:** Execute smart contracts on any supported blockchain via [Analog GMP](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp).&#x20;
* **Decentralized nodes:** Incentivized nodes ensure censorship-resistant, reliable automation.
* **Integrated toolkits:** Leverage [Analog Watch](/documentation/developers/analog-watch) and custom logic for advanced workflows.


# Timechain EVM

Deploy Solidity smart contracts directly on Analog's interoperability network—all through a single EVM-compatible runtime. Timechain EVM provides native Ethereum compatibility for the Timechain, allowing you to deploy and execute Solidity smart contracts directly within our secure, high-performance environment.

To get started with **Timechain EVM**, refer to the [**Quickstart Links**](#powered-by-polkavm-technology) below.

***

## **Why Build on Timechain EVM?**

* **Write Once, Deploy Everywhere:** Execute EVM contracts that natively communicate across blockchains via [Analog GMP](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp#quickstart). No bridges, no wrappers—just seamless cross-chain logic.
* **Leverage Familiar Tools:** Use MetaMask, Remix, Hardhat, and your favorite frameworks. Connect to Timechain EVM in minutes with familiar RPC endpoints and Chain ID.
* **Security by Design:** Leverage TSS and Chronicle Node validation to secure EVM operations. Every contract call inherits Timechain’s battle-tested MPC framework.
* **Reduce Costs Dramatically:** Enjoy up to 90% lower gas fees compared to traditional EVM implementations.&#x20;
* **Execute at Lightning Speed:** Experience contract execution that's only 1.3- 2x slower than native code (vs. 10- 50x on standard EVMs).

{% hint style="success" %}

### Not quite ready to **develop applications on Timechain EVM**? You can try [**Analog GMP** ](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/developers-guide/getting-started)or [**OATS**](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/building-with-oats) instead.

{% endhint %}

***

## Connect to Timechain EVM

There are currently two ways to connect to the Timechain EVM:

* **Integration Environment**: This environment allows you to develop on the **Analog's Integration network**. See [**Network Configuration**](/documentation/analog-network/network-information) for more details.&#x20;
* **Local Development Environment**: This environment is set up on your **local machine**. See [**Network Configuration**](/documentation/analog-network/network-information) for more details.&#x20;

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="On the Analog Integration Environment" %}
This guide outlines how to configure MetaMask for the Analog Integration Network and transfer DANLOG tokens to your MetaMask EVM account.

### **1. Configure MetaMask for Analog Integration Network**

1. **Install MetaMask**:\
   Download and install the [MetaMask extension](https://metamask.io/) if not already installed.
2. **Add Analog Integration Network**:
   * Open MetaMask → Click the network dropdown (default: "Ethereum Mainnet").
   * Select **Add network** → **Add a network manually**.
   * Enter these details:
     * **Network Name**: `Analog Integration`
     * **RPC URL**: `https://eth-rpc.integration.analog.one/`
     * **Chain ID**: `12850`
     * **Currency Symbol**: `DANLOG`
     * **Block Explorer URL**: *Leave blank*
   * Click **Save**.
3. **Connect to Analog Integration**:
   * From the network dropdown, select **Analog Integration**.
   * Your DANLOG balance will display `0` (initially).

***

### **2. Transfer DANLOG to MetaMask**

**Prerequisite**: Ensure you have DANLOG tokens in an account on the [Analog Integration Environment](https://polkadot.js.org/apps/?rpc=wss%253A%252F%252Frpc.integration.analog.one#/explorer) on Polkadot.js.&#x20;

1. **Map Your Account**:
   * Go to **Developer** → **Extrinsics** in Polkadot.js Apps.
   * Select your DANLOG-funded account.
   * Configure:
     * *extrinsic*: **revive** → **map\_account()**
   * Click **Submit Transaction** → Sign.
2. **Transfer DANLOG**:
   * In **Developer** → **Extrinsics**:
     * *extrinsic*: **revive** → **call()**
     * Parameters:
       * `dest`: **Your MetaMask EVM address** (e.g., `0x818F...058b3`).
       * `value`: DANLOG amount (in smallest units, e.g., `50000000000000000` for `5000` DANLOG).
       * `gasLimit`: `100000000`
       * `storageDepositLimit`: `100000000`
       * `data`: `0x`
   * Click **Submit Transaction** → Sign.
3. **Verify Transfer**:
   * Check your MetaMask wallet. The DANLOG balance should update within moments.

{% hint style="warning" %}
**DANLOG** is the native token of the Analog Integration environment. It's needed to cover **gas fees** when you deploy and interact with smart contracts. A public faucet to claim DANLOG directly to MetaMask (for EVM use) is not yet available. We'll announce when it launches.
{% endhint %}
{% endtab %}

{% tab title="On Local Timechain Node" %}
For more advanced development and testing, you might want to run a local Timechain node.

**Prerequisites**

1. **Rust & Substrate**: Install via the [official Substrate guide](https://docs.polkadot.com/develop/parachains/install-polkadot-sdk/).
2. **Git**: Ensure Git is installed.

***

**1. Set Up the Timechain Node**

**Clone and build the repository**:

```bash
git clone https://github.com/Analog-Labs/timechain.git
cd timechain
git checkout development  # Switch to the development branch
```

**Run the node**:

```bash
RUST_LOG="error,evm=debug,sc_rpc_server=info,runtime::revive=debug" \
cargo run --release --features testnet --bin timechain-node -- --dev
```

* **`--dev`**: Starts a fresh development chain.
* Wait for logs confirming the node is running.

***

**2. Start the ETH RPC Proxy**

**In a new terminal**:

```bash
git clone https://github.com/paritytech/polkadot-sdk
cd polkadot-sdk
git checkout polkadot-stable2503  # Use the specified version
```

**Run the proxy**:

```bash
RUST_LOG="info,eth-rpc=debug" \
cargo run --release -p pallet-revive-eth-rpc -- --dev
```

***

**3. Connect MetaMask**

Add a custom network in MetaMask:

* **Network Name**: `Local Timechain`
* **RPC URL**: `http://127.0.0.1:8545`
* **Chain ID**: `12850`
* **Currency Symbol**: `LANLOG`

Save and select "Local Timechain" in MetaMask. Your initial balance will be `0 LANLOG`.

***

**4. Fund Your MetaMask Account**

**A. Map your account**:

1. Open the [Polkadot.js Apps interface](https://polkadot.js.org/apps/?rpc=ws%253A%252F%252F127.0.0.1%253A9944#/explorer).
2. Navigate to **Developer > Extrinsics**.
3. Select your funded account.
4. Choose:
   * **pallet**: `revive`
   * **extrinsic**: `map_account()`
5. Submit and sign the transaction.

**B. Transfer LANLOG tokens**:

1. In **Developer > Extrinsics**:
   * **pallet**: `revive`
   * **extrinsic**: `call()`
2. Enter:
   * `dest`: Your MetaMask address (e.g., `0x818F4D269759Bf180709e7eBbaeF94b2651058b3`).
   * `value`: Amount in smallest units (e.g., `50000000000000000` for `5000` LANLOG).
   * `gasLimit`: `100000000`
   * `storageDepositLimit`: `100000000`
   * `data`: `0x`
3. Submit and sign.

**Verify**: Check your LANLOG balance in MetaMask.
{% endtab %}
{% endtabs %}

## Deploy Your First Contract

Let's deploy your first contract on the Timechain EVM using Remix.

### Prerequisites <a href="#prerequisites" id="prerequisites"></a>

Before you begin, ensure you have the following:

* [MetaMask installed](https://metamask.io/) and [connected to the Analog Integration network](#on-the-analog-integration-environment). Alternatively, you could use a [locally running development node](#on-local-timechain-node).
* A [funded account](#id-2.-transfer-danlog-to-metamask).

***

### Procedure

1. Go to the Remix IDE: [**`https://remix.polkadot.io/`**](https://remix.polkadot.io/#lang=en\&optimize=false\&runs=200\&evmVersion=null)
2. Create a new file (e.g., `Storage.sol`) in the `contracts` folder.
3. Paste the following simple Solidity contract:

```solidity
// SPDX-License-Identifier: MIT
pragma solidity ^0.8.0;

contract Storage {
    uint256 private value;

    function store(uint256 newValue) public {
        value = newValue;
    }

    function retrieve() public view returns (uint256) {
        return value;
    }
}
```

1. Compile the contract:
   1. Go to the "**Solidity Compiler"** tab (left sidebar) and compile the contract (ensure the compiler version matches the pragma).
2. Deploy the contract:
   1. Navigate to the "**Deploy & Run Transactions**" tab.
   2. In the "**ENVIRONMENT**" dropdown, select "**Injected Provider - MetaMask**". Ensure MetaMask is connected to the correct network and your desired account is selected.
   3. Ensure the correct account is selected in MetaMask and Remix.
   4. Ensure "**Storage**" is selected in the "**Contract**" dropdown.
   5. Click the "**Deploy**" button.
   6. MetaMask will pop up asking you to confirm the transaction and show the gas cost. Confirm the transaction.
3. Wait for the transaction to be confirmed.&#x20;
4. Once confirmed, the deployed contract will appear under "**Deployed Contracts**" in the Remix "**Deploy & Run Transactions**" tab.

{% hint style="warning" %}
If you're using a local node and restart it, you'll need to reset your MetaMask nonce. Here's how:

1. Go to **MetaMask Settings**.
2. Click on **Advanced**.
3. Select **"Clear activity tab data."**
   {% endhint %}


# FAQ

<details>

<summary><strong>What is PolkaVM, and why is it used instead of a regular EVM or WASM?</strong></summary>

PolkaVM is a relatively new, high-performance virtual machine based on the RISC-V instruction set architecture, designed for blockchain environments. It has been chosen for Timechain EVM due to its potential benefits in areas like execution speed, compilation/startup time, and a simpler instruction set validation process compared to WASM for certain smart contract use cases. pallet-revive is leveraged to compile Solidity code (via YUL) to run on PolkaVM, thus providing EVM compatibility.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>How is Timechain EVM different from other EVM-compatible chains?</strong></summary>

Timechain EVM distinguishes itself through its integration within the broader Analog network, which is designed as a decentralized liquidity network. Its use of PolkaVM as the execution engine, rather than a direct fork of `go-ethereum` or a WASM-based EVM interpreter, is also a key differentiator.

This architecture aims to leverage Substrate's modularity and security, with a future goal of enabling direct interaction between Solidity contracts and native Substrate primitives, as well as Analog's cross-chain functionalities.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What is pallet-revive, and why is it important?</strong></summary>

`pallet-revive` is a Substrate pallet (a runtime module) that integrates the revive compiler into the Timechain. The revive compiler is responsible for taking Solidity smart contract code (specifically, its intermediate language YUL) and transforming it into RISC-V instructions that can be executed by PolkaVM. It is the critical component that bridges the Solidity language and Ethereum tooling with the PolkaVM execution environment on the Timechain, enabling EVM compatibility.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Is Timechain EVM production-ready?</strong></summary>

Not yet. Timechain EVM is currently in beta and intended primarily for testing, exploration, and gathering feedback. While it has not yet undergone formal audits and isn’t recommended for applications managing significant value, it’s actively being improved based on community input. Users are encouraged to experiment with it in non-critical environments and share their feedback with the Analog team.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>What is DANLOG, and how is it different from TANLOG or ANLOG?</strong></summary>

The Analog Integration Environment uses DANLOG as its native gas token for EVM transactions, configured with 12 decimals in MetaMask. This 12-decimal precision is a key difference from the 18-decimal precision often found in other EVM networks. Remember this when handling DANLOG to avoid incorrect value representation in transactions and smart contracts. Note that DANLOG is distinct from TANLOG (Analog Testnet) and ANLOG, which apply to the Mainnet.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Which EVM tools can I use with Timechain EVM?</strong> </summary>

Currently, [Remix IDE](https://remix.polkadot.io/#lang=en\&optimize=false\&runs=200\&evmVersion=null\&version=soljson-v0.8.28+commit.7893614a-revive-0.1.0-dev.12.js)[ ](https://remix.polkadot.io/#lang=en\&optimize=false\&runs=200\&evmVersion=null\&version=soljson-v0.8.28+commit.7893614a-revive-0.1.0-dev.12.js)is fully supported for smart contract development and deployment. Support for Hardhat is experimental and under active development. Support for other tools like Truffle or Foundry may be considered in the future as the platform matures.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Where can I get DANLOG tokens for the Integration Environment?</strong></summary>

Information on obtaining test DANLOG for the Analog Integration Environment (e.g., through a faucet or from designated pre-funded accounts) will be provided by the Analog team through their official communication channels. For local development nodes started with the --dev flag, accounts like Alice, Bob, etc., are typically pre-funded with DANLOG.

</details>

# Technology & Architecture

## &#x20;Overview

Timechain EVM natively incorporates the Ethereum Virtual Machine using [`PolkaVM`](https://github.com/paritytech/polkavm) and  [`pallet-revive`](https://github.com/paritytech/revive) module. This enables developers to deploy and run Solidity smart contracts directly on Timechain, improving interoperability with Ethereum tools while benefiting from Substrate's modular design and security.

Timechain EVM enables Ethereum-compatible smart contract execution through these core components:

***

### **1. Timechain Runtime**

The foundation of the [Timechain](/documentation/developers/protocol-overview#the-timechain-analogs-core), built with Substrate SDK. It includes:

* **NPoS Consensus**: Secures the network via Nominated Proof-of-Stake.
* **Governance Modules**: Facilitates decentralized decision-making.
* **Forkless Upgrades**: Updates without network splits.
* **EVM Module**: Integrates EVM functionality directly into the runtime.

***

### **2. Pallet-Revive (Execution Layer)**

A custom Substrate module that powers EVM operations:

* Executes contracts as [RISC-V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RISC-V) bytecode via [PolkaVM](https://github.com/paritytech/polkavm).
* Manages contract deployment, state changes, and transaction processing.
* Processes Ethereum-style transactions relayed through the RPC Proxy.

***

### **3. PolkaVM**

A high-performance virtual machine replacing the traditional EVM:

* Built on [RISC-V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RISC-V) architecture for speed and security.
* Features sandboxing for safe contract execution.
* Compiles Solidity code into RISC-V bytecode for compatibility.

***

### **4. Revive Compiler**

Bridges Solidity development with [PolkaVM](#id-3.-polkavm):

* Converts YUL (generated by Solidity compilers) into PolkaVM-ready bytecode.
* Supports multiple Solidity versions; integrates seamlessly with tools like Remix.
* Most compilation steps are automated, minimizing developer effort.

***

### **5. Ethereum RPC Proxy**

Enables Ethereum-native tooling integration:

* Translates standard Ethereum RPC calls (e.g., from MetaMask) into Substrate-compatible transactions.
* Returns responses in Ethereum JSON-RPC format for seamless compatibility.
* Allows developers to use familiar tools without modifications.

## Why Timechain EVM?

**I**ntegrating [PolkaVM](#id-3.-polkavm) capabilities into the Timechain allows you to:

* **Utilize Familiar Tools:** Leverage your existing Solidity and Ethereum development skills and tools, including `Remix`, `Hardhat`, `Foundry`, `ethers.js`, and `MetaMask`, with minimal adaptation for Timechain EVM.
* **Build Unified Applications:** Seamlessly combine EVM smart contracts and native Timechain features within a single application.
* **Enable Enhanced Interoperability:** Create contracts that can interact with core Timechain functionalities, e.g., cross-chain messaging (via [Analog GMP](https://docs.analog.one/documentation/developers/analog-gmp)).
* **Simplify Development:** Reduce complexity by building integrated applications that eliminate the need for separate cross-chain bridges or multi-chain deployments.
* **Access the Analog Ecosystem:** Deploy EVM contracts that benefit from the security and connectivity of the Analog network.

## How It Works

At a high level, the Timechain EVM allows you to treat the Timechain like any other EVM-compatible blockchain (e.g., Ethereum, Polygon, or BNB Chain) for deploying and interacting with smart contracts.

When you send an EVM transaction (e.g., deploying a contract, calling a function, etc.) to the Timechain EVM endpoint, the Timechain runtime processes it. An integrated component, powered by [`PolkaVM`](#id-3.-polkavm) and facilitated by [`pallet-revive`](#id-2.-pallet-revive-execution-layer), takes your EVM transaction, executes the corresponding bytecode within a sandboxed environment, manages the contract's state, and updates the Timechain's storage accordingly.

This process is handled entirely within the Timechain's block production and finalization mechanism, just like native Substrate transactions. The fees for EVM transactions are paid in the Timechain's native token ($ANLOG) and are metered using a gas system analogous to Ethereum, but mapped to Substrate's fee structure.


# Protocol Overview

Analog aims to create a **Decentralized Liquidity Network** that securely connects various blockchain networks. It utilizes a central, custom-built blockchain called the Timechain to facilitate communication between different chains, thereby making the process more secure and streamlined.

***

## The Timechain: Analog's Core

The **Timechain**, also known as the "Accountability Layer," is the foundation of Analog's architecture. Built using the **Substrate SDK**, it acts as the central record for all network activities, ensuring that cross-chain messages are processed securely.

#### Key Features of the Timechain:

* **Substrate-Based**: The Timechain is built on the Substrate SDK, which enables a flexible design, forkless upgrades, fast transaction processing, and robust security.&#x20;
* **Nominated Proof-of-Stake (NPoS) Consensus**: **Time Nodes** secure the Timechain by validating and adding new blocks to the network, ensuring its integrity and decentralized nature.
* **Centralized Coordination (Hub-and-Spoke Model)**: The Timechain is the "hub" through which all messages and data between connected blockchains ("spokes") are routed. This design simplifies integration, streamlines communication, and enhances security by providing a single, trusted point of coordination.
* **Accountability Layer**: The Timechain keeps a complete record of all cross-chain operations, validator registrations, network shard statuses, and governance decisions. This provides transparency and a verifiable history of all network activities.

{% hint style="success" %}
Although the Timechain is built on Substrate SDK and leverages some of the platform’s features, including *forkless upgrades*, *fast block times*, and *security-focused development*, it remains an independent blockchain. For more details about the Timechain and its architectural components, check out the  [Timepaper](https://analog.docsend.com/view/pmud4x28inadu5y3).
{% endhint %}

***

## Chronicle Nodes and Shards: Enabling Cross-Chain Communication

To manage the complexity and scale of cross-chain communication, the Timechain leverages **Chronicle Nodes** and a **sharded network structure**.

**Chronicle Nodes:**

These nodes are responsible for facilitating cross-chain communication by performing two main services:

* **Attestation Service**: Chronicle Nodes monitor, validate, and post inbound cross-chain requests from external blockchains to the Timechain.
* **Relaying Service**: They relay verified cross-chain messages from the Timechain to their destination chains for execution.

Each Chronicle Node has a **Connector instance** that acts as an interface with specific external blockchains, allowing them to participate in Multi-Party Computation (MPC) processes.

**Shards:**

To improve scalability and efficiency, the Analog network is divided into **shards**, which are subnetworks or clusters of Chronicle Nodes that collectively manage cryptographic operations.

* **Task Distribution**: Shards enable the network to distribute tasks among smaller groups of Chronicle Nodes, allowing for parallel processing of cross-chain requests.
* **Specialized Functions**: Different shards can be assigned specific roles or types of tasks, such as handling communication with a particular blockchain or a specific type of message.
* **Improved Throughput**: By dividing the workload, shards significantly increase the network's overall transaction processing capacity, allowing for higher volumes of cross-chain messages.
* **Enhanced Security (Threshold Cryptography)**: Shards use **Multi-Party Computation (MPC)**, specifically **Threshold Signature Schemes (TSS)**. This means that a minimum number of members within a shard must collaborate to perform actions like signing a cross-chain message. This threshold security mechanism enhances fault tolerance and prevents single points of failure. The TSS keys are aggregate keys distributed among these permissionless shards, which then control gateway smart contracts on the connected blockchains.

***

## Gateway Smart Contracts

External blockchains interact with the Timechain and participate in Analog's decentralized liquidity network by deploying [**Gateway Smart Contracts**](/documentation/developers/analog-gmp/overview/architectural-components). These smart contracts act as the on-chain interface between the external chain and the Timechain's Chronicle Nodes.

* **Message Submission**: Applications on external chains send cross-chain messages or token transfers to their local Analog Gateway contract.
* **Message Reception & Dispatch**: The Analog Gateway contract on the destination chain receives verified messages from the Timechain and dispatches them to the target smart contract or address on that chain.
* **Security Integration**: The Gateway contracts are designed to work seamlessly with the TSS-secured operations managed by the Chronicle Nodes and the Timechain.

***

## Timechain EVM: Enabling Solidity Smart Contracts

To further enhance interoperability, Analog integrates **Timechain EVM**, an embedded EVM execution environment directly within the Timechain runtime. This allows developers to use familiar Ethereum tools and write smart contracts in Solidity.

Timechain EVM uses two key technologies:

* **PolkaVM**: A lightweight, high-performance VM optimized for Substrate-based blockchains. PolkaVM compiles smart contracts (including Solidity) into optimized machine code, leading to faster execution and lower transaction fees.
* **pallet-revive**: A Substrate module that provides the embedded EVM execution environment within the Timechain runtime. **`pallet-revive`** allows the Timechain to natively process and execute PolkaVM-compiled smart contracts, acting as a bridge between Ethereum-like transactions and the Timechain's runtime.&#x20;

Check out [Timechain EVM](/documentation/developers/timechain-evm) for more details.&#x20;

# Overview

## Node Roles

Nodes in an Analog network can assume one of two roles:

* **Time Nodes**: Nodes that fulfill the roles of either ***Validators*** (i.e., participating in consensus and block creation) or *Full Nodes* (i.e., providing blockchain data storage and query services). For more details, refer to our [Timepaper](https://www.analog.one/Analog-Timepaper.pdf).  Explore the links below to learn more about running nodes:

{% content-ref url="/pages/3h21Er3d4J4l45cRE0JP" %}
[Running a Validator on the Testnet](/documentation/node-operators/infrastructure-setup/running-a-validator-on-the-testnet)
{% endcontent-ref %}

{% content-ref url="/pages/UrB7Q0NEYeLjVSsNUZrl" %}
[Running a Full Node on the Mainnet](/documentation/node-operators/infrastructure-setup/running-a-full-node-on-the-mainnet)
{% endcontent-ref %}

{% content-ref url="/pages/a8lQgeGImws02c2McGrQ" %}
[Running a Validator on the Mainnet](/documentation/node-operators/infrastructure-setup/running-a-validator-on-the-mainnet)
{% endcontent-ref %}

* **Chronicle Nodes:**  Dual-service nodes that provide both ***attestation*** (monitoring and validating cross-chain requests via TSS before posting them to the Timechain) and ***relaying*** (forwarding Timechain requests to destination chains). For more details, refer to our [Timepaper](https://www.analog.one/Analog-Timepaper.pdf).

{% hint style="info" %}
While Analog will initially manage the Chronicle Nodes to ensure network stability, the long-term plan, as detailed in the [Decentralization Roadmap,](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/decentralization-roadmap) is to shift to a decentralized Chronicle Node network.
{% endhint %}

## Networks

There are two networks, each with its own purpose:

* **Analog Timechain**: This is the main network (mainnet) where the actual $ANLOG token is involved.&#x20;
* **Analog Testnet**: Node operators can freely experiment and test their setups on Analog Testnet, which mirrors the Analog Timechain's protocol and mechanisms, using the TANLOG token available from the [Faucet](/documentation/analog-network/getting-started/analog-testnet-faucet).

## Steps You Need To Take To Operate a Node on Analog

We recommend [Running a Validator on the Testnet](/documentation/node-operators/infrastructure-setup/running-a-validator-on-the-testnet) first to get comfortable with the process and check your hardware and setup is perfect. After familiarizing yourself with the Testnet setup, try [Running a Full Node on the Mainnet ](/documentation/node-operators/infrastructure-setup/running-a-full-node-on-the-mainnet)for a preview of the mainnet experience.

If you’re ready to [Run a Validator on the Mainnet](/documentation/node-operators/infrastructure-setup/running-a-validator-on-the-mainnet), you’ll need to:

* Set the session keys.
* Bond the minimum required amount of ANLOG tokens. Check out [here](https://polkadot.js.org/apps/?rpc=wss%3A%2F%2Frpc.timechain.analog.one#/staking/targets) for more details.
* Sync and monitor your node.
* Maintain your node to ensure the software is up-to-date and be compliant with the best security practices.  &#x20;

The following chapters outline the technical requirements and processes of operating a node as a Validator and Full Node on Testnet and Mainnet.


# Requirements

## **Prerequisites**&#x20;

Before operating a validator on the Analog network, ensure you meet the following requirements:

1. **System Administration Expertise:**
   * Ability to manage server infrastructure, troubleshoot technical anomalies and optimize node performance.
   * Experience with monitoring tools, maintaining high uptime, and applying critical updates.
2. **Secure Infrastructure:**
   * Implement robust security measures to protect your node from breaches (see [*Security Best Practices*](/documentation/node-operators/security-best-practices) for more details).
3. **Start with Testnet:**
   * Begin on the [Analog Testnet ](/documentation/node-operators/infrastructure-setup/running-a-validator-on-the-testnet)to gain hands-on experience, test configurations, and minimize risks before transitioning to [Mainnet](/documentation/node-operators/infrastructure-setup/running-a-validator-on-the-mainnet).
4. **Staking Requirements:**
   * A minimum of **100,000 $ANLOG** is currently required to be whitelisted as a validator.
   * However, to be elected into the active set and earn rewards, ensure you meet the minimum bond requirement. See [Minimum Bond Requirements](#minimum-bond-requirements) for more details.&#x20;

{% hint style="info" %}
This threshold will change as the protocol decentralizes. See [Decentralization Roadmap](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/decentralization-roadmap) for more details.&#x20;
{% endhint %}

5. **Slashing Risks**

* Misbehavior (e.g., downtime, equivocation) or infrastructure failures may result in **slashing**, leading to a loss of staked $ANLOG.
* If uncertain about maintaining reliable uptime, consider **nominating** $ANLOG to a trusted validator instead.

## Technical Requirements

{% tabs %}
{% tab title="Mainnet Beta" %}

## Reference Hardware

Timechain’s transaction weights are based on specific hardware benchmarks tested on AWS’s [c6i.xlarge](https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/c6i/). To process blocks efficiently, we recommend using hardware that meets or surpasses these benchmarks.

**CPU:**

* x86\_64 architecture
* 8 physical cores

{% hint style="warning" %}
The process must meet the following specifications:

* Intel - Ice Lake or newer (Core or Xeon series)
* AMD - Zen3 or newer (EPYC or Ryzen).
* A processor selection with emphasis on [single-threaded performance](https://www.cpubenchmark.net/singleThread.html) over a higher core count.
  {% endhint %}

**Memory:**

* At least 16 GB

**Storage**:

* At least 300 GB storage (NVMe SSD). It is worth noting that the size of the Timechain and its ecosystem will grow over time, making storage scaling essential.

**Network**:

* A minimum symmetric network speed of 500MBps is required.

{% hint style="danger" %}
While a less powerful machine might seem like an option, it is not a gamble you want to entertain due to the inherent risk of performance.
{% endhint %}

## Reference OS

* The Timechain's binary files have been compiled and tested on `Ubuntu 22.04 LTS`.  However, the steps should be similar for other Ubuntu versions, such as `18.04 LTS` or `20.04 LTS.`&#x20;
  {% endtab %}

{% tab title="Testnet" %}

## Reference Hardware

Timechain’s transaction weights are based on specific hardware benchmarks tested on AWS’s [c6i.xlarge](https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/c6i/). To process blocks efficiently, we recommend using hardware that meets or surpasses these benchmarks.

**CPU:**

* x86\_64 architecture
* 8 vCPUs

**Memory:**

* At least 16 GB

**Storage**:

* At least 300 GB storage (NVMe SSD). It is worth noting that the size of the Timechain and its ecosystem will grow over time, making storage scaling essential.

**Network**:

* A minimum symmetric network speed of 500MBps is required.

## Reference OS

* The Timechain's binary files have been compiled and tested on `Ubuntu 22.04 LTS`.  However, the steps should be similar for other Ubuntu versions, such as `18.04 LTS` or `20.04 LTS.`&#x20;
  {% endtab %}
  {% endtabs %}

## Minimum Bond Requirements

To become a validator on the Analog network, you must first be whitelisted by holding at least 100,000 $ANLOG. After whitelisting, you'll need to be elected into the active validator set to start producing blocks and earning rewards. This requires staking a minimum bond, which includes enough $ANLOG tokens for a stash with the [existential deposit ](/documentation/analog-network/getting-started/wallets-and-accounts#existential-deposit)and transaction fees.&#x20;

While nominators can contribute to your stake, you'll need a base amount. For instance, as of March 25, 2025, the minimum stake to join the active set was 10.9425 million $ANLOG. You can find up-to-date figures via this [link](https://polkadot.js.org/apps/?rpc=wss%3A%2F%2Frpc.timechain.analog.one#/staking/targets).&#x20;

<figure><img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FnLoFqoi9B1rjryXNcfbj%2FMinimum%20Bond.png?alt=media&amp;token=46dbf602-79b9-4969-a03d-4e4aeb4cb58c" alt=""><figcaption></figcaption></figure>


# Decentralization Roadmap

Analog aims to fully decentralize through a phased/staggered approach. The following roadmap details the process of decentralizing network validation within the Analog ecosystem. As of ***January 15th,  2025***, phase 1 of the roadmap is currently being executed.

## Phase 0: Pre-mainnet stage

The pre-mainnet network primarily relied on Analog nodes to perform Time Node and Chronicle Nodes services. There were no inflationary rewards during this stage, and Analog’s nodes did not receive any rewards for participating in the TSS and NPoS consensus.

## Phase 1: PoA Network

The first stage of decentralization will involve a limited set of validators: Analog nodes and whitelisted nodes from the Incentivized Testnet. Community participation in validation services such as [Time Nodes](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/overview#timechain-nodes) or [Chronicle Nodes ](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/overview#chronicle-nodes)will not be permitted during this stage.&#x20;

In this phase, there will be no inflationary rewards, and Analog nodes or whitelisted Time Node operators will not receive any incentives for their participation in the TSS and NPoS consensus mechanisms.

## Phase 2: Staked Time Nodes

In this phase, Analog will enable a staking mechanism. Whitelisted operators and Analog nodes providing [Time Node services](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/overview#timechain-nodes) can stake their tokens to earn rewards for participating in the NPoS consensus.

Community members/token holders can also nominate/delegate their tokens to whitelisted operators or other Analog nodes, earning inflationary rewards proportional to their nominated stake. Analog will still run [Chronicle Node services](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/overview#chronicle-nodes).

## Phase 3: Decentralized Chronicle Nodes

This phase will involve decentralizing Chronicle Nodes by allowing third-party entities to participate as Chronicle Node operators. To ensure the continuous provision of attestation/relaying services for supported chains, Analog will maintain a majority node presence while gradually granting operational authority to a rotating group of whitelisted operators.

## Phase 4: Community-run Validation

This phase will mark the final stage of Analog’s decentralization from a validation standpoint. Analog nodes will no longer enjoy any special privileges in the validation process, regardless of their role as Time Nodes or Chronicle Nodes.

The ANLOG token will assume the central role in critical decision-making, encompassing staking and the future direction of the Analog network.


# Infrastructure Setup

Running Analog infrastructure is essential for network security and performance. As an Analog node operator, you have the opportunity to run several key services:

* **Full Nodes**: to support the core functionalities, such as archive, RPC, etc.
* **Validators**: to participate in the NPoS consensus and contribute to block creation on the Timechain.&#x20;
* **Chronicle Nodes (Coming soon)**: to provide vital attestation and relaying services.

Your choice of service within the Analog ecosystem will be influenced by your technical expertise, goals, and available resources. This section walks you through setting up and connecting a Timechain Node, covering both full node and validator configurations.

## Quick Links

# Running a Full Node on the Mainnet

{% hint style="info" %}
The Analog Mainnet commenced a soft launch on ***December 23, 2024***, initially operating with a select group of authorized validators. This controlled phase will precede the official launch, marked by the Token Generation Event (TGE) on ***January 15, 2025***.&#x20;

The network is designed to evolve gradually towards a decentralized model, featuring validator selection through a Nominated Proof-of-Stake (NPoS) mechanism and governance entirely driven by on-chain token voting. For more details, see [Decentralization Roadmap](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/decentralization-roadmap).
{% endhint %}

This section explains how to install the full node on the Analog Main-net Beta Release.

## Getting Started

You can install and run a full node using any of these three options:

<details>

<summary><strong>Option 1: Running pre-built Docker Image</strong></summary>

### **Prerequisites**

* [Docker](https://docs.docker.com/get-docker/) or [Podman](https://podman.io/) installed on your machine.
* [Minimum hardware requirements](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/technical-requirements#technical-requirements)

### **1. Install the Timechain Node Image**

Pull the latest Timechain image from DockerHub:

* If you are using D*ocker,* run:

```bash
docker pull analoglabs/timenode-prod:latest
```

* If you are using *Podman,* just run:

```sh
podman pull docker.io/analoglabs/timenode-prod:latest
```

### **2. Run the Timechain Node**

Use this command to start the node:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
docker run -p 9944:9944 -p 9615:9615 \  
  -v /path/to/data:/root/.local/share/timechain-node/ \  
  analoglabs/timenode-prod:latest \  
  --name 'YOUR_NODE_NAME' \  
  --rpc-external \  
  --prometheus-external  
```

{% endcode %}

**Flags Explained**:

* `-v /path/to/data:...`: Persists chain data to your local directory. Replace `/path/to/data` with your preferred storage path.
* `--name`: Assign a unique name (e.g., `analog_node_01`) for identification on [Analog Telemetry](https://telemetry.analog.one/).
* `--rpc-external`: Enables external access to the RPC server.
* `--prometheus-external`: Exposes metrics for monitoring.

{% hint style="warning" %}
When running the Timechain Node in Docker, it listens only on localhost by default. To enable external connections for services like RPC and Prometheus, use the `--rpc-external` and `--prometheus-external` flags. For interface details, consult the built-in help (`--help`) or the official documentation for [flags](https://docs.substrate.io/reference/command-line-tools/node-template/#flags), [options](https://docs.substrate.io/reference/command-line-tools/node-template/#options), and [subcommands](https://docs.substrate.io/reference/command-line-tools/node-template/#subcommands).
{% endhint %}

### **3. Sync Chain Data**

The node will automatically start syncing with the network as shown here:

<img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FNygYNJmjiAV0HU19vlYJ%2FRunning%20Full%20Node%20on%20the%20Timechain.png?alt=media&amp;token=9def380c-5d31-4580-9036-7d7a37f4c25d" alt="" data-size="original">

This process may take several hours, depending on the network bandwidth and your hardware.

**To check sync progress**:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
docker logs --tail 100 [CONTAINER_ID] | grep "Syncing"  
```

{% endcode %}

Replace `[CONTAINER_ID]` with your container’s ID (find it using `docker ps`).

### **4. Configure RPC Settings**

Customize RPC access using these flags in your `docker run` command:

| Flag                    | Purpose               | Example                              |
| ----------------------- | --------------------- | ------------------------------------ |
| `--rpc-methods`         | Restrict RPC methods  | `--rpc-methods safe`                 |
| `--rpc-max-connections` | Limit connections     | `--rpc-max-connections 100`          |
| `--rpc-cors`            | Manage access control | `--rpc-cors all` (allow all domains) |

For advanced configurations, refer to the [RPC documentation](https://paritytech.github.io/devops-guide/guides/rpc_index.html?#important-flags-for-running-an-rpc-node).

### **5. Verify Node Operation**

#### **Check Node Status**

1. **Logs**:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
docker logs -f [CONTAINER_ID]  
```

{% endcode %}

2. **RPC Endpoint:**

Test your node's RPC endpoint by sending a request:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
curl -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"id":1, "jsonrpc":"2.0", "method":"system_health"}' http://localhost:9944
```

{% endcode %}

A successful response indicates your node is operational.

### **6. Monitor Node Metrics**

#### Setting Up Prometheus

1. Create a `prometheus.yml` configuration file:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
global:
  scrape_interval: 15s
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'analog_node'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['localhost:9615']
```

{% endcode %}

* Restart Prometheus.

2. Run Prometheus with Docker:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
docker run -d -p 9090:9090 -v /path/to/prometheus.yml:/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml prom/prometheus
```

{% endcode %}

#### Setting Up Grafana

1. Run Grafana with Docker:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
docker run -d -p 3000:3000 grafana/grafana
```

{% endcode %}

2. Access Grafana at `http://localhost:3000`
3. Add Prometheus as a data source:

* Go to Configuration > Data Sources
* Add Prometheus with URL `http://localhost:9090`
* Click "Save & Test."

4. Import a dashboard for node monitoring:

* Go to Dashboards > Import
* Import a Substrate/Polkadot dashboard (you can find templates on Grafana's website).

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Option 2: Running pre-built binary</strong> </summary>

Coming soon.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Option 3: Install via Debian repository</strong></summary>

Coming soon.

</details>

Contact technical support on [Discord](https://discord.com/channels/860069399627825163/1242304290740244561) and share your feedback.


# Running a Validator on the Mainnet

{% hint style="info" %}
To participate in the NPoS consensus protocol for the Analog Network, you must deploy and run a validator and, optionally, a [Timechain full node](/documentation/node-operators/infrastructure-setup/running-a-full-node-on-the-mainnet). The Analog Main-net commenced a soft launch on ***December 23, 2024***, initially operating with a select group of authorized validators.

This controlled phase will precede the official launch, marked by the Token Generation Event (TGE) on ***January 15, 2025***. The network is designed to evolve gradually towards a decentralized model, featuring validator selection through a Nominated Proof-of-Stake (NPoS) mechanism and governance entirely driven by on-chain token voting. For more details, see [Decentralization Roadmap](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/decentralization-roadmap).
{% endhint %}

This section outlines the detailed steps for installing, running, and maintaining a validator on the Analog Main-net.

## Prerequisites

See [Requirements](/documentation/node-operators/introduction/technical-requirements#prerequisites) for more details.&#x20;

## Getting Started

You can install and run the Timechain Node using any of these three options:

<details>

<summary><strong>Option 1: Running pre-built Docker Image</strong></summary>

### Prerequisites

1. &#x20;[Docker](https://docs.docker.com/get-docker/) or [Podman](https://podman.io/) installed on your machine.
2. Minimum bond amount of **$ANLOG** tokens (check the current requirement [here](https://polkadot.js.org/apps/?rpc=wss%253A%252F%252Frpc.timechain.analog.one#/staking/targets)).

### Step 1: Install and Run the Timechain Node

#### 1.1 Pull the Docker Image

```bash
docker pull analoglabs/timenode-prod:latest
```

If you are using *Podman,* just run:

```sh
podman pull docker.io/analoglabs/timenode-prod:latest
```

#### 1.2 Start the Node

Run the following command to start your validator node:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
docker run -d -p 30333:30333 -p 9944:9944 \
-v  /path/to/data:/root/.local/share/timechain-node/ analoglabs/timenode-prod:latest \
--name 'INSERT_YOUR_NODE_NAME' rotateKeys --validator \
--unsafe-rpc-external \
--rpc-methods=unsafe
```

{% endcode %}

**Key Flags Explained:**

* `-v /path/to/data:...`: Persists chain data to the host directory (`/path/to/data`). Replace this with your preferred path.
* `--name`: Sets your node’s display name on [Analog Telemetry](https://telemetry.analog.one/).
* `--unsafe-rpc-external` and `--rpc-methods=unsafe`: Enable RPC interactions (use only in secure environments).

### Step 2: Sync Chain Data

Your node will **sync the full chain history** by default. To use **Warp Sync** (faster), add `--sync=warp` to the command in Step 1.2:

{% code lineNumbers="true" %}

```bash
- "--port=30333"
- "--rpc-port=9933"
...
- "--sync=warp"
```

{% endcode %}

Example command with **`Warp Sync`** enabled:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
docker run -d -p 30333:30333 -p 9944:9944 \
-v  /path/to/data:/root/.local/share/timechain-node/ analoglabs/timenode-prod:latest \
--name 'INSERT_YOUR_NODE_NAME' rotateKeys --validator \
--unsafe-rpc-external \
--rpc-methods=unsafe
--sync=warp
```

{% endcode %}

Wait until your node is fully synced. Monitor progress via logs:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
docker logs -f [CONTAINER_ID]
```

{% endcode %}

### Step 3: Bond $ANLOG Tokens

After syncing, bond $ANLOG tokens to activate your validator:

1. Go to [Polkadot.js Apps](https://polkadot.js.org/apps/?rpc=wss%3A%2F%2Frpc.timechain.analog.one#/explorer) and connect to Analog Timechain:
2. Navigate to **Network>Staking > Account Actions**.
3. Click **+ Stash**:

<img src="https://3751219372-files.gitbook.io/~/files/v0/b/gitbook-x-prod.appspot.com/o/spaces%2FMINNcjmm2i5Xcankmyyd%2Fuploads%2FqS7PzjI5MGJSkf2d5Jdm%2FBonding%20Preferences.png?alt=media&amp;token=c610ef68-832c-4b67-843e-acbd1149e66f" alt="" data-size="original">

* **In the modal window:**
  * Select your **Stash account** (the account with your $ANLOG tokens)
  * Enter the amount to bond in the **Value bonded** field
  * Choose your **Payment destination** for staking rewards

4. Submit the transaction and sign with your wallet.

### Step 4: Set Session Keys

Session keys link your node to your controller account:

#### 4.1 Generate Keys

Call the RPC method `author_rotateKeys` on your running node:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
echo '{"id":1,"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"author_rotateKeys","params":[]}' | websocat -n1 -B 99999999 ws://127.0.0.1:9944
```

{% endcode %}

This returns a hex-encoded key string (e.g., `{"jsonrpc":"2.0","result":"0x0cef998a81357e73e79e2ed98e1d3ff81dfabf55444c14e3291ca9beaebfe9754483d09abf1021358280713b90fb96497ac6a2f22575f8421ffd7287fc1225440e521f344f34afc396bd4ee26164ce1962957d2a553f0d968a02068863c08375","id":1}`)&#x20;

The string is the concatenation of the three public keys. Save this result for a later step. If you do not have Websocat installed, read this [article](https://lindevs.com/install-websocat-on-ubuntu) to learn more about how to install it.

#### 4.2 Submit Keys via Polkadot.js

1. Go to **Network>Staking > Accounts Actions**.
2. Click on **Set Session Key** for the bonding account you set earlier.&#x20;
3. Paste the key string from Step 4.1.
4. Submit the transaction.

Your node is now registered as a validator candidate.

### Step 5: Verify Node on Telemetry

Check if your node is active on the network:

1. Visit [Analog Telemetry](https://telemetry.analog.one/#list/0x1459b0204b92719ffc978c5da3d6a2057973916bd548f8076df2064bc1cb4cfc).
2. Search for your node name (set via `--name` in Step 1.2).
3. Confirm it shows as **"Active"** under the validator list.

### Step 6: Monitor Your Validator

To monitor detailed metrics (CPU, memory usage, chain height, peer count), use Prometheus and Grafana:

### Prometheus Setup:

* Install Prometheus ([official docs](https://prometheus.io/docs/prometheus/latest/installation)).
* Add the flag `--prometheus-external` to your Docker command to expose metrics on port `9615`.
* Configure `prometheus.yml` with targets pointing to your Analog node metrics endpoint.

Example configuration snippet:

{% code overflow="wrap" %}

```bash
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'analog-validator'
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['localhost:9615']
```

{% endcode %}

### Grafana Setup:

* Install Grafana ([official docs](https://grafana.com/docs/grafana/latest/setup-grafana/installation)).
* Add Prometheus as data source in Grafana:
  * Navigate to **Settings > Data Sources > Add Data Source**.
  * Select Prometheus; set URL (`http://localhost:9090`)
* Click Save & Test

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Option 2: Running the pre-built binaries</strong></summary>

Coming soon.

</details>

<details>

<summary><strong>Option 3: Install via the Debian repository</strong></summary>

Coming soon.

</details>

## Support

Contact technical support on [Discord](https://discord.com/channels/860069399627825163/1242304290740244561) and share your feedback.
